ParlaMint-NO_2017-06-13.ana.ud206e223	This is a question of waiting wages and retirement benefits for former grossing-up representatives and what to deduct. So far, wages and salaries or income have had to be deducted from post-paid wages and salaries, while capital income has been excluded. However, in the autumn of 2016, the Storting adopted a new law on compensation for grossing-up representatives. In dealing with this issue, the Stortinget decided to ask the presidency to determine how capital income could be included in the system of curtailment of retirement benefits and reimbursement of the representatives — this in line with other income. The Presidency then requested the appointed Regulatory Committee to follow up this Decision and the Regulatory Committee issued its report on 29 May 2017. It is a unanimous committee which proposes rules for the deduction of capital income from retirement benefits and remuneration. The Presidency considers that the rules on curtailment of capital income, as in the case of curtailment of other income, apply to both retirement and post-employment benefits. Both benefits are intended to be a security of financial security if the representative has challenges to get into work. This consideration is not without prejudice to the fact that the representative has capital income during the period in question. The introduction of a rule on curtailment of capital income requires the establishment of detailed rules on the income considered as capital income and on the timing of the income. The Presidency, like the Regulatory Committee, proposes that the provision be designed in such a way as to reduce capital income which is to be regarded as taxable income during the performance period. The Presidency underlines that capital income exempted from taxation will not form the basis for shortening the remuneration and retirement benefits. Furthermore, only capital income paid during the performance period that will be the subject of curtailment will be included. The Presidency also considers that the principle of equal treatment between common forms of savings means that interest income should also be exempted from the curtailment rule. The Presidency will propose that this Act enter into force on 1 January 2018 and will therefore not apply to retirement benefits until the autumn of 2017.

ParlaMint-NO_2017-06-13.ana.ud206e309	This has been an easy matter to be the case rapporteur for, because it is a matter that most people agree on. Those who have their own comments will themselves present their own arguments for these. The purpose of the Norwegian-Swedish Electricity Certificate Scheme is to contribute to the increased production of electricity based on renewable sources in both countries, and so has it. The agreement sets an overall renewable electricity production target in Norway and Sweden of 28,4 TWh in 2020, to be triggered by the common electricity certificate market. Other control stations have been completed and the resulting changes are good for Norway and there is reason to be satisfied with the work done. It is important that it is possible to correct for any estimation deviations faster than current practice, and it is better that the allowances are now regulated. The government shall now, at least every two years, provide for adjusted electrical certificate allowances, thereby correcting any deviations as quickly as possible, which is desirable. However, it is also important that an assessment be made in the intervening years as to whether allowances should be adjusted and that this assessment be published as soon as possible after the assessment evidence is available, in order to prevent uncertainties about allowances adjustments. The law imposes decisions on adjustments at the latest by the end of the year before the effect of the adjusted allowances on the scope of the obligation to issue an electric certificate. Determination late in the year before the start of the delivery year may cause problems for those who need knowledge of production and consumption for calculations. Power suppliers need to know the allowances in a reasonable time before the start of the delivery year. This is a problem for which understanding needs to be understood, and it is important that the best possible transparency of processes and assessments is sought, and that relevant information is published as early as practicable in order to provide market participants with predictability around allowances adjustments. The Government proposes that, as a general rule, adjustments resulting from new knowledge of the previous years be incorporated into the allowances for the following year, possibly also the following year, and that these adjustments may exceptionally be incorporated into the electricity licence allowances for up to four consecutive years. This is a good proposal that is supported. The changes resulting from the second control station are good changes and other control stations have brought about changes in the Norwegian-Swedish electricity certificate market that are important for the Norwegian energy market. We're happy with that.

ParlaMint-NO_2017-06-13.ana.ud206e348	As the rapporteur has explained, since 2012, Norway and Sweden have had a single market for electricity certificates. The system has served as an aid scheme for the development of new renewable energy production. We are well on track to achieve the common goal of 28.4 TWh as renewable power generation in Norway and Sweden as a result of this scheme in 2020. So far, as of April of this year, 18.3 TWh has been built up in connection with this scheme. About two thirds of this is wind power. For the time being, Sweden has had the biggest contribution to what has been built out, but Norway is coming well, albeit a little late. There are many projects under development now, which will be operational by 2021, and investment decisions have been made on a number of other projects that also have the potential to achieve targets by 2021. So the goal for Norway, the original goal of 13.2 TWh, is the great probability that we can reach. When Stortinget addressed the energy message in the summer of 2016, it became clear that a large majority in this room would not set new targets for the electricity certification system after 2020. To the representative Heikki Eidsvoll Holmås, who then addressed this issue, I stress that there was a broad political consensus in the Stortinget about not setting new goals. The bottom line is that in Norway we have a surplus of renewable energy in production compared to consumption. To take the results of 2016 specifically, we had a production of 148 TWh and a consumption of 132 TWh, i.e. a profit of 16 TWh. In addition, the forecasts for the coming years are that there will still be a surplus in the Norwegian market. It provides the basis for the phase-in of new consumption both in the transport sector and in new industries, e.g. data storage centre, which has a high potential in Norway. The fact that we have not set new targets in Norway does not make any difference in this matter. What we're seeing now is Sweden has set a new target of 18 TWh towards 2030. From my point of view, it has been important to create a predictable framework for our Norwegian power producers, our Norwegian builders in both hydropower and wind power, in this system. Especially this was important in winter, when we had a demanding price picture for electric certificates. The main framework for this market is set out in the original agreement between Norway and Sweden on a single market for electricity certificates. However, on 5 May this year, a new change agreement was signed with Sweden, which we on the part of the government took a lot of time to arrive at, which assumes that Sweden has set a new target and will continue with 18 TWh towards 2030, so we will end by 2021. I am very happy that we came to an agreement with Sweden. It ensures predictable framework conditions for Norwegian builders. This is a district industry with many large and small builders in both wind and hydropower, and it is often landowners in the primary industry, farmers, who are involved in the development projects. The new agreement is good for Norway. It allows us to finance all new projects that will be completed by the end of 2021 in this common electricity licence market. This means that we can also go beyond the original 13.2 TWh that was the goal for Norway. This means, in practice, that we can get into a situation – if we have a large expansion in Norway in the coming years – which means that Swedish consumers are involved and pay for investment in new Norwegian wind and hydropower development. This has received a very positive response in the industry, in the renewable market. Both the Small Powers Association and the Wind Powers Association have given the new agreement diced six. So to the concrete proposal that is now on the table, linked to the other control station. The rapporteur has explained the main elements, so I am not going to repeat it, but would stress that there is an important change here, in the sense that the bill now proposes that the Ministry be allowed to set the allowances in regulation, and that the law specifies the key principles on which to determine and subsequently change the allowances in this regulation. A similar legislative amendment is being discussed in the Swedish Parliament, so that we now have equivalent systems on both sides of the border. That amendment will simplify the administrative process of adjusting the electricity licence allowances in line with the framework provided by Stortingen in this case. It gives market players good and necessary predictability. I note with satisfaction that the Committee today adheres to these principles, so that we can achieve the best possible, and the most efficient, market for the electricity certification system in the period to which this applies, until 2035, for Norwegian consumers and Norwegian water and wind generators.

ParlaMint-NO_2017-06-13.ana.ud206e419	Several have not asked for the floor on case No. 2nd@item: inlistbox
ParlaMint-NO_2017-06-13.ana.ud206e435	From time to time, the Energy and Environment Committee is visited by parliamentarians from other countries. There are some questions that remain in all these meetings: how have you managed to get as much out of the energy resources that Norway has? How have you managed to keep control? How have you been able to ensure that the energy resources benefit the entire people? The answer to this is that we have a concession law that aims to secure precisely these conditions. On 23 July 1909, the predecessor of today's industrial concession law, the acquisition law, was adopted in Stortinget after 13 full days of debate. The importance of hydropower for industrialisation was thoroughly discussed. The right to return was crushed. The public interest should be safeguarded. The workers who built a power plant were to be protected. Landowners and municipalities were to be compensated for interference and the interests of the State were to be safeguarded. The representatives were deliberately the importance of hydropower for future industrial development. Left-man Kristian Friis Petersen said it like this: "... there is every reason to believe that the development which is taking place in this respect will, in the course of not so many years, have completely changed all industry, and it will have created a number of industries in places where they were not before; it will completely recalibrate industry and the life-style of the workers' people." In 1917, the law was finalized by the industrial concession law and the watercourse regulation law of 1917. So it is a law over 100 years old that we are now updating with regard to form and language themes. It is a law that has served us well. Our hydropower resources have provided a basis for industrial travel across the country. The value creation has benefited the public, and it is undeniable that these resources belong to the community and are to be disposed of for the benefit of the community. When it became apparent in the 1960s that we as a nation also had large oil and gas resources on the base, a law was drafted for this sector, which was largely passed on to the industry concession law. The resources belong to the community, they are to be exploited for the benefit of the community and benefit the whole country. There's been a lot of water going through the turbines since 1909 and 1917. Our country has changed. New issues have emerged on the agenda. Old issues have become irrelevant. The law has been amended to capture developments, as appropriate. The principles of disagreement in 1909 have become largely political joint ownership. Hydropower is the backbone of our renewable power system and as such will have a major impact on the development of Norway as a future zero-emission society. It is therefore important that we always have an up-to-date law that governs the development of this sector in the direction we want. Although the main principles are underpinned, there will also be political disagreements for the future about how these principles are to be practised and new issues that need to be regulated. The proposition we are dealing with today is intended to simplify and update the law by editing and updating the legal texts in a language. The intention is not to change the legal state or to make material changes. This has been the basis of the Committee's unanimous support for the amendments proposed. I would like to thank the Committee for its good cooperation.

ParlaMint-NO_2017-06-13.ana.ud206e481	As the rapporteur, I would like to thank the entire Committee for contributing to a good and orderly attitude, where it is easy to relate to the positions of the various parties. The starting point for today's debate is Prop. 110 L for 2016-2017, which deals with changes to the Planning and Building Act and the Matrikkel Act. This is the most recent of many changes made by the government that will simplify procedures for municipalities and contribute to more efficient planning processes. I was going to go through some of the changes and how the different parties have made comments and suggestions. There is broad agreement on a new provision to introduce infrastructure measurement and documentation requirements in the ground. It is important that both planners and developers have easy access to this information, while recognising the need of individual owners to protect some of their information. The majority of hearing bodies also recommended such a requirement. The type of wiring and other infrastructure to be registered is of a more technical nature and is to be laid down in the Regulation. There is also broad support to introduce a mandatory requirement for regional planning forums by changing the law from ‘should’ to ‘shell’. The centre party and SV have some additional suggestions about who should be invited, and whether the meetings should be open. Relevant zones were included in the Law on Planning and Building as a separate concept in 2008. The proposal proposes to include a new area of consideration for the preservation of mineral resources for possible future value creation, supported by a majority in the Committee. The proposal to introduce a time-limited change of use as a new measure is underpinned by an overall committee. The amendment of the wording of the Planning and Building Act from the Ministry to ‘give’ regulations concerning the measures exempted from the requirement of responsible undertakings and the obligation to apply to the Ministry to ‘give’ such regulations is supported by the Government Parties and the Christian People's Party and the Left. A clarification that the deadline for the framework authorisation shall be 12 weeks, while the period for the amendment of the authorisation, including the framework authorisation, shall be three weeks, supported by all except SV and the Green Party on the Environment. There are several proposals an overall committee is behind, and on which I will not spend more time in this post. There is some disagreement about amendments on impact assessments, which I will briefly explain. As a follow-up to an EU directive, penalties are now in place for breaches of the rules on impact assessments. The Central Party and the Christian People's Party have an amendment to the legislative text which states that the breach must be grossly negligent and not only negligent. The Labour Party, SV and the Green Party oppose the proposal to simplify the rules on impact assessment requirements by shifting the provision from law to regulation. The size has previously decided to require impact assessments for peat take-offs below 2 million. cubicle or for ants less than 1500 Dec. This is followed up in the proposition by the reduction from 1500 to 200 Decars of the limit of time at which peat harvesting is always to be carried out. The centre party here has its own proposal to lower the limit to 800 dean. Then I have been through most of the proposals and can well establish that there is a broad consensus on the main purpose behind the proposition, namely to facilitate more efficient planning processes and easier procedures for municipalities.

ParlaMint-NO_2017-06-13.ana.ud206e1121	Long-term and central social interests are linked to ownership and management of soil and forest. The Christian People's Party believes that society should have control and influence over the ownership and trading of agricultural property. But I don't find so many good bridges from the professional analyses to many of the government's political proposals. Therefore, there is also little support for the government from the Christian People's Party in this matter. One of the observations that worries me is the increase in land rent. We can say that over the past 25 years, there has been a huge gap between the ownership and business structure of agriculture. The proportion of land rented is high throughout the country. One very serious problem, as I see it, is that too much of the agricultural area is about to go out of business. In my opinion, the regulations of the Land Act on compulsory employment are the main tool for ensuring the operation of all land and thus keeping the land in claim and ensuring active farmers access to land. I'm glad that the whole committee joins in this. The obligation to drive applies to all land and all ownership time. The follow-up of the obligation to operate lies with the municipalities, and I think they need more sanctions to enforce this basic obligation as part of the ownership of agricultural land in Norway. I am concerned that the farmers should not lie awake at night because they are not sure whether rented land can be included in a long-term basis for investment and recruitment. Leases must therefore be long-term and written. Furthermore, incentives for the sale of additional soils need to be strengthened. I lack the government's response to the proposal of the working group that assessed the land problem in 2015 and that pointed to tax as an effective tool for the sale of additional land. I have faith in this instrument. The Christian People's Party strongly reiterates the function of the law to ensure good and forward-looking solutions for the design of property. The rules of the Land Act on the sharing of property shall therefore always apply, including for the sale of additional land. In my opinion, the proposal on changes in the landline of the concession obligation is not well-founded. The concession law has important functions to ensure the settlement and healthy formation of prices on agricultural property. I would warn against undermining the authority of this regulatory tool by limiting its scope to the extent that it is now set up. In parts of the country, large parts of the agricultural area will fall under the proposed concession exemptions. Nor can I see a single valid argument that this proposal will reduce the rent share. Nor does the Christian People's Party support the proposal for a concession waiver in cases where the tenant buys property. This is an unnecessary proposal, as we see it, because existing legislation and the practice of it already facilitates the sale of additional land when good operational solutions can be achieved, and when the price reflects the return value of the properties, driven by agriculture. The best can be the enemy of good. I remind myself that all regulation and interference in private property rights should have a very good purpose. In terms of price control on clean forest properties, I have been in doubt. Forestry lives under market conditions other than agriculture and is characterised by long-term investment and rarely harvested. Nor has forestry had such a development in business structure as agriculture. Price control is also not a suitable tool to ensure a scattered and local ownership of forest resources. Such considerations lie in other functions of concessional treatment. On the other hand, most of the forest land in the country is mainly farmland. This, together with land and grazing, forms the basis for most of our farms. Therefore, the Christian People’s Party cannot support the changes in the legislation that could pose a risk that a new price level for clean forest land could pass on to farmland, and thus to general agricultural property. Such an approach suggests that changes in price control of clean forest properties must be extremely cautious. The Christian People's Party will therefore not support the proposal to exempt clean forest property from price control during concession treatment.

ParlaMint-NO_2017-06-13.ana.ud206e1689	We can, of course, agree that the draft law we are discussing in this matter has not marked the political agenda over time. There is also, then, a unanimous committee which supports the proposal for a new law prohibiting the admission or use of information on specified areas, displayable objects and soil conditions. The Committee also points out that the proposal is essentially a continuation of the prohibitions in the temporary law on the protection of spatial information. The new legislative proposal suggests that information on areas, objects and bottoms can only fall within the prohibition if the information can cause harm to the security of the state, the independence of the state or other vital national security interests. Except that protective objects can also be subject to the law, the proposal will therefore be an embellishment of today's law. In addition, rules are laid down which will clarify the framework for the prohibitions. The proposal is inter alia: justified by the desire to restrict information sharing to the extent that it is safe to do. The key in regulatory work will be to find a solution that balances the interests of civil society and the national need for secrecy. The Committee points out in its observations that the legislative proposal has been out of the question and notes that the hearing bodies have been divided and had partly conflicting views on the proposal. It is therefore appropriate to mention that in this respect the Committee has also noted that the Ministry has made clarifications in the final legislative proposal and its observations and has complied with the objections to the proposal. The Committee has also noted that the question of whether the scope of the law should include Svalbard, Jan Mayen and the bee countries wishes to be investigated by several central hearing bodies. The Committee supports the proposal for a further investigation but asks that the Stortinget be properly informed before any regulation on the application of the law to Svalbard, Jan Mayen and the coun tries is developed. Finally, I would like to point out that today’s law is a temporary law and is valid until 1 October 2017. For this reason, it is important to deal with this law today, so that after 1 October we also have a law to deal with the matter. On this basis, a unanimous committee concludes its opinion on the proposal.
ParlaMint-NO_2017-06-13.ana.ud206e1708	The government is proposing a new law on information about specified areas, displayable objects and soil conditions. The proposal substantially continues the prohibitions in the Temporary Act on the protection and control of spatial information in the interests of national security. At the same time, the government's legislative proposals in a number of areas will involve liberalisation and the decoration of the current ban. In addition, the government has proposed the establishment of a prohibition on the recording and use of information on specified display objects. The purpose of the law is to protect and exercise control over information that needs to be protected in the interests of national security and other vital national security interests. Where safe, the law shall also facilitate the use of such information for socially important purposes. I am pleased that a unanimous Committee on Foreign Affairs and Defence has joined the government's proposal for a new law. I have also noticed that the Committee has asked for the Stortinget to be briefed on whether the law will apply to Jan Mayen, Bouvet Island and Norwegian beeland in Antarctica. The Government will inform the Storting appropriately before such a regulation is adopted. Technological developments are progressing rapidly, and the ability to obtain, process and exploit information is increasing. This has a major impact on a number of socially important activities and offers new opportunities for the private sector. It's a desired development. At the same time, information that can be used for important societal and business purposes, also by foreign powers, would be in conflict with our national security interests. One example is detailed information on the bottoms of the Norwegian coast, which in a crisis situation could be of crucial importance in providing information to Norwegian military forces. The screening of this type of detailed information is essential to avoid that foreign powers can operate with high precision in Norwegian waters. The security policy situation has become more unpredictable and demanding. Today, we are facing a more complex risk and vulnerability picture. In this respect, I have noticed that the Committee has underlined the need to maintain a clear and defined ban on taking up and using information on bottom-up conditions that are deemed to be relevant to the security of the State. In the further regulatory work, the government will clarify the detailed framework for the prohibitions in the law. This work involves strict balances between partly conflicting considerations affecting a number of public and private actors. Provision will be made for maximum information to be used for societal purposes, unless this information does not harm national security interests. ParlaMint-NO_2017-06-13.ana.ud206e1743	This is a matter which has been discussed several times by the Storting, both in the context of the Committee's proposals and through the submission of this proposal. This Regulation shall enter into force on the twentieth day following that of its publication in the Official Journal of the European Union. The proposers propose that the EOS control directive be repealed and that the provisions of the instructions be integrated into the EOS control law. The proposals for material changes to the legislation are based mainly on the proposals of the Evaluation Committee, as set out in Documents 16 for 2015-2016. Some linguistic adaptations have been proposed in the legal text, without any changes in reality. And as I said when I presented the bill: in some areas, the proposal for a representative does not address changes proposed by the evaluation committee. It is highlighted and discussed in the debate and at the presentation, so I shall not repeat it here. But what I thought I would do was link some comments to the sample's inspection and transparency, since there's been a lot of discussion about it here. It has also been raised in consultation opinions to the Committee of the Departments, and it has been discussed in the press. Source protection is important to have an operational EOS service. That is why I perceive that the EOS Committee, the E-service and the PST take this equally seriously. And so does the Storting. So why is the Committee not in favour of the Ministry of Justice's proposal that particularly sensitive information in the PST should be excluded from control? Is it not natural to treat the PST and E services in the same way? Well, as the Committee sees it, it is difficult to call such an exercise a non-discrimination when it comes to two different services, two different missions, two different mandates, two different ways of facilitating control and not at least two different information management systems. In the consultation round, the EOS Committee also expressed its opinion. They wrote to the Supervisory Committee that the proposals of the Ministry of Justice and Defence have been a step backwards for the democratic control of the PST. This has also been highlighted in contributions from the head of the EOS Committee in the evening post. In its comments on the precautionary measures adopted by the Committee, the Committee considers that the EOS Committee complies with its controls. For the purpose of the verification, the identity of the source is entirely irrelevant. The control needs relate to completely different things than who the source is. That is why it is so important to maintain the possibility of control. The need for control concerns the need to record data on the related source. The need for control concerns the need to record negative information on the source itself. This concerns the need to store data on supervised persons that are not considered relevant for monitoring purposes but are stored in a source guidance system to manage the source guidance as a method. It concerns whether the source of the service crosses the illegal provocation limit and whether the source is in line with the internal procedures of the PST. In my time in the Control Committee, where I have been responsible for following up the EOS Committee, the annual reports have taught me that this control is something that PST and all the EOS services want to welcome, and they facilitate it. It's a nobility of our secret services. The internal legislation of the PST on sources has been graduated and, as sources, persons are considered to be hidden and in strict confidentiality reports on the activities of persons and groups. It follows from the proposal for Section 8 that the sample shall, as far as possible, take into account the protection of sources and the protection of data received from the rest of the world. It must be assumed that the sources of the PST, as opposed to the intelligence service, which is most likely to be Norwegian, and that the information collected will apply to the Nordics to a large extent. The Evaluation Committee suggests that the use of sources by the Intelligence Service appears well organised today for the monitoring of the use of sources by the EOS Committee. The selection may have full access to information from and about sources, with the exception of particularly sensitive information. The proposers point out that, following the evaluation committee’s view, the EOS Committee should have access to the information on the source of the PST, as reported in the SourceSys registry. The evaluation committee indicates that the reasons for the PST’s wish to keep information which may reveal the identity of the sources of the service, outside the scope of the EOS Committee, are generally linked to the risk that sources will stop sharing information with the service if it cannot provide the sources with an unconditional promise of anonymity. These are arguments put forward by the PST and presented by the evaluation committee. This has never happened. There has never been any indication that this has been the case, but there is a concern that the PST travels, and that the Minister of Justice has repeated several times, last in an article in the Evening Post on Saturday. In a letter to the Committee, the Minister for Justice and Security writes: ‘The law must ensure that the transparency of the EOS Committee does not prevent the access of services to important information. In order to ensure access to information from human sources, it is essential that the sources of services can rely on the fact that their identity cannot become known to unauthorised persons. If sources cannot trust it, the access of services to information from individuals will be very unfortunate. [[...]] Without security of sources, services will not be able to carry out the societal mission.’ No one disagrees with this. Source protection is as important in the PST as in the E service. But it is the service itself that has not found to be able to facilitate the system so that the EOS Committee can access information about the sources of services without getting to know their identity, and it is the responsibility of the Council of State to ensure that it is prioritised. When it comes to the issue of the right of access to information of the intelligence service, the proposers refer to the Innst. 146 S for 2016-2017, as follows: ‘The majority of the Committee, all with the exception of the member of the Socialist Left Party, considers that the restriction of the transparency of ‘special sensitive information’ must be resolved either by granting access to a single EOS selection or by maintaining the current regime, based on the 1999 decision of the Storting. The majority believe that the current system of unupgraded transparent criteria for which parts of the intelligence service activities other than ongoing democratic control should be continued.’;» It is a unanimous committee that reproduces this. The Socialist Left Party is the only ones outside the comment. Proposals indicate that the Ministry of Defence approved in 2014 the following classified definition of ‘especially sensitive information’. It's important to be clear, especially when we're talking about particularly sensitive information within two different types of services. The particularly sensitive information in the E service is about: the identity of the human sources of the E service and foreign partners; the identity of the specially protected officials of foreign partners; persons and operational plans in the holding standby; Special sensitivity of e-services and/or foreign partners to foreign operations such as compromise seriously damage the relationship to foreign power due to the political risk of the operation; or may cause serious injury or loss of life to its own personnel or third parties; The exemption does not limit the transparency of information on and from Norwegian and foreign sources in general by the EOS Committee. As a general rule, the sample is given access to such information in such a way that the identity of the sources is anonymous by giving each source a unique number, or otherwise. In some cases, such anonymisation would not be sufficient to conceal the identity of the source, for example: because other information about the source or the contact of the service with him would be suitable for revealing the identity. If, in such cases, the identity of the source is considered to be particularly sensitive, the information on and from the source will be totally excluded, subject to the above-mentioned derogation. Source protection is essential for an operational service and the Committee is committed to ensuring it. Another reason why we are concerned with maintaining the current system is that the checks carried out by the EOS Committee against the PST have proved necessary. They have led to important changes that have provided a safer service and that have given better control. We welcome that. A unanimous committee, all the parties to the committee, is of the opinion that the protection of sources is currently functioning as intended. Sources cooperating with the PST and with those on mission for the Intelligence Service may be confident that their identity is well protected. That is why I think it is problematic when the Minister of Justice, in his hearing opinion, implies that it is not necessarily safe to do so. He also writes that in the Evening Post. I think it is problematic when the Minister of Justice himself is questioning the source protection of the PST. That's where the problem lies. In the Ministry of Justice and Defence's proposal for legislative change, it appears that the intention of the proposal is that the identity of human sources in the PST should not be available to the EOS Committee. But it is not today, and there is no wish for that information either. The democratic control of the use of sources concerns the use of methods and the reason for registration. It's not about identity. A unanimous committee indicates that it is the responsibility of the Council of State to enable the services to carry out their functions in a manner which makes it available for control, while at the same time ensuring the protection of sources. Therefore, it is a unanimous committee asking for priority to be given to this work and for the PST register to be set up in such a way that the verification of methodology can be carried out without revealing identity. It is a unanimous and collective committee that is of the opinion that today's right of access works as intended, in order to provide for the protection of sources and the need to monitor the use of methods, and we want to maintain it.

However, if the Storting joins the sample’s view, the PST will no longer be able to clear the reports for identifying information before the Secretariat and the sample reads them. This poses an unacceptable risk of identity becoming known. In the PST, there are only a few who have access to the source meeting reports, simply because experience suggests that the more people who know, the greater the danger that the source's identity may come out. It poses a real risk to the life and health of these sources. For ethical reasons, the PST cannot use a source if it endangers life and health for sources itself, for family members or for others. In PST, only the few informants have a special need for protection called sources. All the others are called contacts, and for them, the EOS Committee has full access. Thus, when giving the source status to each individual, it has been assessed that it would be important if they were to provide PST information. It is inherent that those persons who have relevant information for the PST have access to environments that do not want the PST to know about them or their tasks. We know that these environments can be very dangerous. Of course, the sources themselves will also be more restrained or withdrawn when the PST can less guarantee anonymity. It will have a significant negative impact on the ability of the service to carry out its societal mission. The PST depends on the information to implement prevention, investigation and government-enforcement measures. If access to source information is weakened, we will have to accept that the PST will be less able to solve the societal challenge. There is simply no way to ensure good enough intelligence without the information from sources. Therefore, I hope that the PST will be able to provide reassuring protection to its sources in the future as well. We need all the information they can provide.

ParlaMint-NO_2017-06-13.ana.ud206e1968	No one has asked for the floor.
ParlaMint-NO_2017-06-13.ana.ud206e1984	I thank the Cooperation Committee on this matter, which deals with changes to the Police Register Act in order to implement EU Directive 2016/680. There is a joint committee which gives its opinion on the matter. This Directive regulates the processing of personal data by police and prosecution authorities for criminal purposes and the processing of personal data in the framework of police cooperation and other judicial cooperation in criminal matters. The Directive does not entail substantial substantive changes in the Law on the Registration of Police. The amendments proposed mainly focus on clarifications and additions or are related to procedural rules. The greatest difference is the scope. While the rules previously applied to the exchange of personal data between countries, the Directive also applies to the national processing of data. Norway has already implemented this mainly in its legislation. Following the proposal, further clarifications of existing definitions will be added to the legislative text. At the same time, some new definitions are added, including: on biometric personal data. It is also proposed to distinguish between different categories of persons when processing personal data in criminal matters. The provision on the provision of data to the rest of the world adds that conditions for the supply of data to countries which have not implemented the Directive are laid down in the Regulation. It is also proposed that the data subject should be able to sue the Data Protection Supervisor, the controller and the processor without prejudice to any other administrative appeal procedures. Furthermore, a number of minor changes are proposed to the Archive Act, the Police Act and the Criminal Procedure Act and the correction of an error in the Criminal Code that is not linked to the implementation of the Directive. It is proposed, inter alia: the requirement to provide police attestations for staff members of the supervisory authority is made mandatory.

ParlaMint-NO_2017-06-13.ana.ud206e2012	The changes are largely of a legal nature. There is not so much policy in the laws themselves, and it has not been the biggest debate in the committee, but let me relate a few words to what a unanimous committee is proposing to amend the law. These amendments harmonise the rules on passport and national ID card issuance, which are planned to be introduced during 2018. The proposed changes are also an adaptation to the introduction of new systems and procedures for issuing passports and national ID cards. I'm glad we're about to get national ID cards in place, and these changes are necessary to prepare for the implementation of national ID cards. In particular, there is a need for more flexible rules that provide more room for regulation through regulation. The applicable rules on the period of validity of the passport and the amount of the passport fee are therefore proposed to be replaced by statutory homes, in accordance with the ID card law. The Government's proposed changes to the passport law continue in all respects the law in force, but, like the ID card law, § 11 facilitates the disclosure of data from the passport register when necessary to verify a passport or the identity of the holder. The government also proposes an addition to both the ID card law and the passport law that facilitates the systematic communication of information to the public register about who has been issued passports or national ID cards. In the Committee's position, there is also a comment from the Centre Party, which points out that it is important when the passport fee is to be included in the regulation, that it does not become ‘unreasonable’ high – I think that is the term used in the comment. Now, a total increase over several years has been concerned that fees are to a large extent fixed in line with self-cost, and I believe that this is something that the government and, if necessary, future governments will also generally apply in relation to the type of fees, so that it should not be unduly high. With this I recommend a unanimous setting.

ParlaMint-NO_2017-06-13.ana.ud206e2043	At the request of the Judicial Committee, the President will propose that the speaking time be limited to 5 minutes to each party group and 5 minutes to members of the Government. ParlaMint-NO_2017-06-13.ana.ud206e2052	The Norwegian penal system is one of the mildest in the world. It has a lot of nice sides, and then it has some less beautiful sides. One of the challenges of our system is that, in some cases, there is room for very large penalties. In particular, this is the case in the case of repeated gross impairments of integrity. If you kill one person, the maximum penalty is 21 years. If you kill two or three or five or ten, the theoretical maximum sentence is still 21 years in prison. Fortunately, we have custody of the most cruel acts, the problem of the system lies in the grey zone between the ordinary 21-year sentences and the detention penalty. This is what the proposal addresses here. Our system today is simply not good enough to capture these cases. It can be seen that crimes are not reflected in the punishment, that they do not have a consequence. The solution proposed by the government is simple and good. We want to maintain the 21-year maximum penalty for killing or serious integrity violations, but we believe that several such acts should have a slightly higher penalty framework – not much, but it should be possible to reflect the fact that a serious act has been committed several times. It's so simple, so logical -- it's just about making our system a little bit fairer. Then comes a very significant point that I think some have forgotten in the debate that has been. Increasing the maximum penalty for repeated acts is something we are already doing today, but we are doing it primarily for crimes with the lower penalties. To take an example, theft is punished by two years. If several thefts are committed at once, the maximum penalty will be four years. That way, we double the penalties, but never beyond six years. It's a good system, and it's a logical system. But why can't we do this every day for the most serious crimes that go up to the 21st anniversary? It almost seems as if the 21st anniversary limit is a kind of sacred thing that you don't want to change in Norwegian criminal law, but there's nothing in principle in the way of extending the maximum penalty a little for more crimes that have a 21-year penalty framework. So we know that there has been predictable opposition to the proposal. Several of those who are voiced against are the same forces that want not only the same punishment that we have today, but also considerably lower penalties than what we have today. There are also many of the same cancers that thought it was unfortunate that victims and survivors were given a role in our legal system, among other things. in the introduction of aid lawyers, the same forces that announced that it would be the end of the legal system that we knew, but I think things have gone quite well anyway. It is important to remember that we punish primarily for the perpetrator. But there's no doubt that we're actually doing it a little for the sake of the victim and they're also leaving. Those who persistently claim otherwise should wake up to the political reality – this is a real reason, which I know is behind the desire to punish, both on the right and the left. I see that there is nothing wrong with us as Norwegian politicians being involved in developing and deepening the grounds for punishment. It's not written in stone by legal theorists, it's also a teaching developed in this hall. It's not just something you can research. As elected representatives, we also have a responsibility. Indeed, a human criminal justice institution must, in my view, also take care of the victim and the survivors. They may never get the sense of freedom back. Giving them soul peace for a few years extra matters. That they should not be anxious to meet a perpetrator they fear is important – it has a value. Values are not sensory, and this proposal does not reverse the legal system that we know, as someone has claimed. The Labour Party's own Minister of Justice Knut Storberget said in 2008: ‘Strengthening the rights of the offended and the deceased is central to maintaining trust in the judicial system.’ That is an opinion that I fully agree with. It's just so sad that the Workers' Party today cannot follow up on the intention of its former Minister of Justice. Otherwise, I want to address the suggestions we are on.

Victims must be taken more seriously than they are today when it comes to this type of crime. It is important and right that it is now possible to condemn a higher level of reprisals in cases where people have been subjected to anti-truth crimes by several perpetrators.
ParlaMint-NO_2017-06-13.ana.ud206e2201	This case is about important fundamental boundaries in our criminal justice care. We have, and have always had, a mild society in Norway. One of the reasons for this is that society's criminal responses are humane. We do not have unreasonable punishments in this country, and we do not have unreasonable and poor reconciliations. We're not supposed to have that either. Looking at societies with very high levels of punishment and poor reconciliation, this is often reflected in society as such. It's not that if we have high penalties for, for example. Kill, there's fewer killings, or if we have bad reconciliations, there'll be fewer prisons. In many cases it may seem that the opposite is the case. That's why it's important that we think very carefully about when we move on to the primitive boundary mark. In this case, it has gone reasonably quickly. As I understand it, this case has been delivered after the deadline Stortinget itself has set. Furthermore, it is dealt with urgently within the Committee and several of the hearing bodies were unable to attend hearings and could not therefore be consulted. A case like this requires a more orderly process than it has been put up in this case. At least that's my opinion. In Norway, figures show that crime is on its way down. The rate of relapse is among the lowest in the world. We have good reasons to be proud of the tradition we have in Norway. If the goal is little crime and good rehabilitation back to society, we're really doing pretty well. But it's clear this is a nuanced question. Penalty levels are a difficult question. We have probably all received e-mails with thousands of signatures from a campaign for higher maximum penalties. At the same time, we know that when laymen are sitting in court and going to impose penalties on specific cases, the question is no longer as simple. Then they often judge more mildly than a tradesman would. It is not easy to discuss the level of punishment with those who are victims or relatives after a criminal act. The problem is that it is not possible to make up for the suffering that has been caused. In this case, several argue that as long as one killing can lead to 21 years of imprisonment, more killings should lead to an additional increase to 26 years of imprisonment. But is it really such that an increase from 21 to 26 years of age is what is needed to bring people’s sense of justice into line with the law? Or will this lead to further pressure to increase the level of penalties? The penalty level is not fixed and static. There is something that changes over time, and there are many considerations that need to be weighed against each other when determining the level of penalties. Of course, people's sense of justice is one of these considerations. But we should remember that it is to society that individuals should make up for themselves, and rehabilitation is an important part of the punishment. The sentence is to go back into society and live as normal, as someone's neighbor and someone's colleague. Preventive considerations must be taken into account. In those cases, the preventative effect is less than for other types of crimes. It is often affected inside the picture, and the preventative effect of a punishment is decreasing when the punishment reaches a certain level. There are two reasons why the Left does not support the government's proposals for criminal atrocities. Firstly, it is because the process has not been in line with the seriousness of this matter. But the important thing is that we want to continue the line we have in Norwegian criminal justice, with humanity and moderation as carrying values. With regard to the government's proposal to amend the Damage Compensation Act § 3-5 and § 5-3 in order to clarify the possibility of setting separate claims for compensation for the individual responsible, we support it – as others have also stated that they do. I want to promote the proposals of the Left that have been submitted. In principle, we thought that we should vote subsidiaryly for the Labour Party's proposal. However, I am not as sure anymore, because I feel that we have two government parties that argue very strongly for their view of the matter, which is natural, but which also does not indicate that there is any doubt that this is a difficult matter, and that there are some important reasons that need to be balanced against each other. I'm afraid that if a sample is to be set down, it won't be as broad a structure as it should be, given the gravity and importance of the matter.

I note that the majority of the committee has signaled that they want to continue today's high penalties and that unfortunately the majority does not support the government's proposal for increased penalties.

But the question is whether the multiples mean it because they a) know what the actual penalty level is, or b) because they don't know it. And we actually have a lot of knowledge about that. It was a major research project at the University of Oslo in 2010 that carried out a thorough investigation of people's arrests for punishment. What did we learn from that? Well, we learned three things: People want more punishment. People think the penalty level is much lower than it actually is. When they are asked what would be a proper level of punishment, people in general respond less than what is the real level of punishment today. In the case we've been leading, it's all about serious integrity constraints. Okay, what does the research say about it? Well, when people are presented to things about street election and party election, only 36 pst would. give the same severe punishment as a court actually gives. When they were presented for electoral matters, only 43 pst would. give the same severe punishment as that which a court actually gives. And when one asked them what they thought a court would give in punishment, the actual penalty level in all cases was seriously underestimated by the ones they asked. This was an outrage before the criminal atrocities of the new criminal law. The fact is, then, that the Norwegian level of punishment is largely in line with the general perception of law. Yeah, sometimes it's actually overly strict. But just as fully, most people think that the penalty level is much lower than it is. Why is that? Well, maybe it's because we have a bunch of politicians running around in election fighting after election fighting and giving a misleading picture of how hard we actually punish people in this country. They should end with that.

ParlaMint-NO_2017-06-13.ana.ud206e2530	In Norwegian criminal law, perpetrators usually receive both stricter penalties and have to pay higher compensation, the more offensive and serious a crime is. Of course, this is for all thoughtful and upcoming people — the more serious the crime, the more severe the punishment. A more severe punishment means higher compensation for the more traumatised victim. This was supposed to be the starting point in Norwegian courts. Unfortunately, paradoxical and grossly unfair exceptions to this rule exist. A more grotesque crime may in practice lead to a lower level of liability. In 2008, the Supreme Court clearly concluded that a rapist receives a discount on the compensation if he commits this loathsome act together with several others in a group rape. If each of the rapists paid their legal compensation for the victim, the compensation would be NOK 530000. The Supreme Court, on the other hand, believed, against all common sense, that group rapists should get away cheaper because the assault was committed by several. The compensation was therefore cut by well over half and ended at NOK 200,000. The rapists were also made jointly responsible. In practice, this means that if one of them has money on the book and is forced to pay the entire sum, the rest of the rapists get away without paying compensation at all. So I'm very happy that half of this proposition is going through today, that is, removing the so-called group discount. But the government does not get a majority in favour of the proposal to increase the maximum penalty, and I regret that very much. The bill is on the table. It has been developed by the country's leading lawyers in the Law Department, and the government's proposal has been at the hearing since December 2016. It is also quite special that the majority in this Chamber today believe it is entirely right to deal with half of the law today, but it is totally irresponsible to adopt the other half of the proposition. The arguments of the opposition, which has a majority in this case, are that the Storting has had too little time to assess it. But it makes sense in all opposition posts that they would never vote for this increased punishment, no matter how many extra days, weeks, months or years they had. This is not a new debate. There's a debate that's still going on in public space. I think you're very aware of the positions. But I find that the Christian People's Party, the Workers' Party and the Center Party etc. Today, it will support a proposal that will put down another sample and get another investigation. I think this really is throwing blues in the eyes of the victims, who want to get a penal code that is more in line with the sense of justice. I am proud that the Progress Party and the Right, our partner, stand side by side in this matter to take the victims seriously and to propose an increased maximum penalty.

During the hearing, it was emphasised that people’s general perception of the criminal framework in Norway is that they believe the penalties are much lower than they actually are. It was underlined again and again during the hearing. When it's said here that the general idea is the opposite, that's not true. We need to make sure to prevent better for those in prison and to ensure that they can get back to society in a good way. Not least, convictions should be able to come back to work, be good neighbors, be good colleagues, etc. In other countries, it appears that only increasing the penalties framework does not have a good effect. So we see a lot of spaces. This is a serious claim on which the representative from the Left was involved, because this is not about how we are trodden on victims and relatives. This is an important matter, which we must take seriously.

ParlaMint-NO_2017-06-13.ana.ud206e2765	First, I would like to thank the Committee for its good cooperation in the treatment of Prop. 120 L for 2016-2017. Proposition is a follow-up of NOU 2012:17 on Love and Cooling Tower – Criminal issues in case of serious infectious diseases. In the proposal, the government has put forward proposals to raise the blame for gross negligence in the transmission of infectious disease and to change the disease definition to cover the transmission of diseases that cause significant damage to body or health. The Government proposes in the bill that criminal liability will not occur if proper infectious control measures have been implemented. In addition, the circle of who can give a criminal consent is proposed to be extended to any person and not just a consensual or spouse. They have also proposed that before a criminal agreement is to be given, the parties must receive infectious disease guidance together from health professionals. Furthermore, the penal framework for serious transmission of infection has been proposed to be raised from three to six years. In addition, the government has proposed a new penalty clause in Section 238 of the Penal Code on Public Hazardous Dissemination, which is essentially an editorial change. The Government also proposes that the spread of the general threat of terrorist infection be regarded as an act of terrorism, that the removal obligation be extended to cover the spread of the general dangerous disease, and that it be punishable to establish an association for the spread of the general dangerous infection. In general, the Committee has endorsed the proposals made by the government, with the exception of proposals on the pre-criminal consent system, in Section 237 of the Penal Code. The majority, consisting of the Labour Party, the Right Party and the Progress Party – i.e. not the Christian People's Party and the Centre Party – have, following an overall assessment, proposed decisions in the vote, removing the condition that prior to the prior consent of the criminal prosecution one must have received an infectious guidance. The majority goes away from the sample recommendation. The Christian People's Party believes that it should be punishable if the partner has given prior consent, but nevertheless believes it is important that the individual is given good information so that he can make a qualified decision. That is why we are voting against the amendment to Section 237 of the Penal Code in the proposal for Decision III.
ParlaMint-NO_2017-06-13.ana.ud206e2780	I am pleased that the Justice Committee has largely supported the government's proposal for changes to the criminal law and the Criminal Procedures Act. The Government’s proposal follows up an investigation that has assessed the appropriateness of the use of penalties against infectious behaviour. Infective behaviour means infection both directly and indirectly between persons and infection into an indeterminate circle of persons, i.e. spread of infection. Like the sample, the government has concluded that both transmission and spread of infection should continue to be punishable by Norwegian law, while making certain substantive changes. The proposal has generally been supported by the Hearing Officers. On this basis, the government has proposed to continue a criminal order in Section 237 and Section 237a of the Criminal Code on the transmission of serious diseases, but with some substantive changes. The government’s proposals address medical developments in recent years regarding the treatment of HIV infection. Developments have led to HIV-infected people with successful medical treatment no longer posing a real risk of infection. The government’s proposals reflect this by making it clear in the legal text that criminal liability is not imposed where proper measures for the protection of infectious diseases have been taken, for example: for the medical treatment of HIV infection. We also propose to raise the blame from negligence to gross negligence. This is in line with the main provision chosen by the 2005 Penal Code. Furthermore, we propose to change the disease definition to include only diseases that cause significant damage to the body or health. In the proposal, the Government proposed to extend the scope of persons who could give impunity consent to any person, but on the condition that the persons must go together to the disease protection guidance of health professionals before consent can be given. I note that the majority of the Committee is in favour of non-compliance with such terms and conditions of infectious disease guidance. The background to our proposal on infectious disease guidance was to ensure that consents should be sufficiently informed. Ensuring sufficiently informed consent is also important as a result of extending the circle of persons entitled to consent. On the other hand, we did not propose that the actual consent be given under the infection protection guide or entered into the record, as the sample originally provided. I believe that our proposal on the conditions of prior infectious disease guidance provides a balanced solution, but takes note of the views of the majority of the Committee. We also propose a new penalty order in Section 238 of the Penal Code, which targets the spread of infection that poses a general risk to life or health. Such spread is already punishable by the law in force, but it has several advantages to regulate it in its own criminal law. The spread of infectious diseases differs from the transmission of infectious diseases by allowing a wider range of persons to be affected. Individuals will be less able to protect themselves against it and will often do so in business or other organised activities. An individual criminal order provides for the possibility of adapting the detailed terms of punishment to those specificities, while at the same time taking into account the criminal framework that the case may be very serious. In line with this, we are proposing a 15-year penalty framework for intentional infringement and 6 years for negligent infringement. The Proposition also proposes that terrorist infection should be affected by the terrorist code, that it should be punishable to establish a transmissible network, and that the spread of infection should be subject to the criminal deportation obligation. In addition to the amendments to the penal code, we are also proposing a new provision in the Criminal Procedures Act, which strengthens the protection of those who have been subjected to serious sexual abuse. The proposal provides for a routine examination of the infectious status of suspects. This is not the case today. In practice, the new home means that it will be known more quickly than it is today if it has been infected. In this way, we avoid that the insult that has been subjected to serious assaults also has to withstand the additional stress it actually is to go in uncertainty about the infection risk. The proposal received broad support in the hearing, and I am pleased that we are now getting a provision that strengthens the position of the offended in these serious cases.

ParlaMint-NO_2017-06-13.ana.ud206e2828	From time to time it is necessary to adapt the legislation to technological developments. Biometric authentication is becoming increasingly common as an access key to computer systems. According to current legislation, in certain circumstances, police have the power to seize digital material and to carry out physical investigations. These are important tools for detecting and preventing serious crime. We'll probably see even more examples of things that used to happen in the physical world, more and more being transferred to the digital arena. Then I think it is important that we as legislators are conscious of our responsibility and adapt the laws to the facts. Developments have led to a slight coincidence in which the storage medium used determines whether or not the police can in practice seize the material, cf. the 2016 Supreme Court judgment. This creates opportunities for criminals to circumvent the confiscation code, which is unlikely to be the scope of the Storting at the time of the adoption of the confiscation rules. That is the bias in the legislation we have the opportunity to rectify today, and I am pleased that we have a clear majority in this Chamber who agree with the government's assessments in this matter. The setting being presented is on behalf of the Progress Party, the Right, the Christian People's Party and the Center Party. The Labour Party, the minority of the Committee, has requested that these legislative changes proposed by the government be postponed and assessed in so-called wider context, and they refer to the review carried out in the department under the Criminal Procedures Act. It is also a good position to have – as I believe it is the case rapporteur – but we and a clear majority want to make this change of law now, when the government has promoted it, and to give the police this tool as they wish. So there's a broad majority behind the vote, which in this case is promoted by the Advance Party, the Right Party, the Christian People's Party and the Center Party.
ParlaMint-NO_2017-06-13.ana.ud206e2843	Thank you to the President-in-Office for a good review of the matter. I just want to say, on behalf of the Labour Party, that the basis for our voting against is exactly what the rapporteur said, that we want this to be seen in the context of the legal work that is actually going on in the department.
ParlaMint-NO_2017-06-13.ana.ud206e2850	I am pleased that the majority of the Judicial Committee supports the Government's proposals for legislative changes, which will ensure police access to computer systems opened by biometric authentication, where the computer system is subject to search or seizure. From time to time, the issue arises as to whether the police have access to new technologies in the investigation. The legislator cannot anticipate technological developments. Therefore, it is sometimes necessary to adapt the legislation in order to close unintended gaps. The proposal submitted by the government is precisely such an adjustment. The laws will allow the police to require anyone in possession of a computer system subject to search or seizure to open the computer system with fingerprints or other methods of biometric authentication. The legislative changes will also provide the police with a clear basis to impose coercion on those who do not comply with such a biometric authentication requirement. The criminal law requirement to impose and enforce must be necessary and proportionate will, of course, apply. The legislative changes are necessary for the police to have the necessary means to effectively investigate criminal offences. It is becoming more and more common to use fingerprints, iris recognition or other methods of biometric authentication to unlock mobile phones or other computer systems. The content of these types of units may constitute decisive evidence in criminal matters. It is therefore important to ensure police access to computer systems opened by biometric authentication, if necessary by coercion. The legislative changes have their background in a decision of the Supreme Court of August 2016. The Supreme Court concluded that the police in the specific case did not have the authority to use coercion against the defendant in order to get him to open his cell phone with fingerprint authentication. The Government’s proposed legislative changes were supported in the consultation by a majority of the hearing bodies. A majority of the hearing bodies also consider that there is an urgent need to establish a legal framework as proposed. I have noted the views of the members of the Committee from the Labour Party, that the Government ‘Should await the examination of this Bill to the Stortinge to be submitted to the notified overall review and proposals for amendments to the Criminal Procedures Act that are expected to arrive at the Stortinge in a short time’. I agree with these members that it may be challenging to get the overall overview of coercive measures when minor changes are made several times, and that there is a need for a comprehensive review. Such a review will be obtained with the follow-up to the Criminal Procedures Committee’s investigation into new criminal law. However, the work on new criminal law will take some time — but perhaps no more than the members of the Workers' Party see fit when they suggest that treatment in the Storing should take place ‘not long’. But it's urgent to put in place a biometric authentication coercive helmet in situations where a computer system is already being searched or seized. It is not satisfactory to wait for the entry into force of a new criminal law. The government will facilitate police use of the necessary tools to effectively investigate criminal offences, while taking sufficient account of citizens’ protection against tampering. I am pleased that we can now present this proposal.

The penalty shall not be increased by more than 6 years and not more than 21 years, except for an increase after the third paragraph of this point. The sentence is increased to a maximum of 26 years in prison if two or more of the offences are punishable by imprisonment for up to 15 years or more. For persons under 18 at the time of action, the penalty framework is not increased beyond 15 years. The increase in the penalties provided for in this point only applies to legal provisions which have provided for the imposition of a higher penalty framework. (b) Where a previously convicted person has again committed a criminal offence of the same nature as he has previously been convicted in this State or abroad, the penalties shall be increased until twice as long as the offender decides otherwise. The penalty rate is not increased by more than 6 years and not beyond 21 years. The increase in the penalties provided for under this point only applies when the conviction had reached the age of 18 at the time of the previous criminal offence and has committed the new act after the penalty for the previous offence has been fully or partially enforced. If the new criminal offence is punishable for more than 1 year, the penalty shall not be increased if the new offence is committed later than 6 years after the execution of the previous sentence has ended, unless otherwise decided. If the new criminal offence is punishable for 1 year or less, it may not have been more than 2 years after the execution has been completed. The increase in the penalties provided for in this point only applies to legal provisions which have provided for the imposition of a higher penalty framework. (c) Where an offence has been committed as part of the activities of an organised criminal group, the penalties shall be increased until twice. The penalty rate is not increased by more than 6 years and not beyond 21 years, and for persons under 18 at the time of action, not more than 15 years. An organised criminal group means a cooperation between three or more persons whose main purpose is to commit an act punishable by imprisonment for at least 3 years or who assume that a non-negligible part of the activity is to commit such acts. The increase in the penalties provided for in this point shall apply to legal provisions conferring legal effect on the criminal framework, unless otherwise provided.’;»
