ParlaMint-NO_2017-06-13.ana.ud206e7235	Norway’s national human rights institution is a young institution, but important. In our time, when unrest and insecurity characterise many countries and important human rights are subjected to pressure and breaking, it is an important signal from the Stortinget that we want to strengthen the work of human rights by establishing this institution, subject to the Stortinget. It is gratifying to record, and to congratulate, that the institution has now been granted the ‘A’ status of the Board of Directors of the International Network of National Institutes for Human Rights, GANHRI. Thus, the Stortinget’s assumption that the institution should comply with the international principles for the status of national institutions, the so-called Paris principles, is fulfilled. The Committee is in agreement in its position, and I would like to thank the members of the Committee for their good cooperation in order to arrive at a unified understanding of how the Stortinget can respond to the recommendations of the Norwegian National Institute for Human Rights in the best possible way. I would also like to thank the Norwegian National Institute for Human Rights for good information and dialogue on the challenges the institution points to. Cooperation and follow-up will develop over time and, there will be scope for good further dialogue on how the Storting can best follow the recommendations of the institution in the years to come. Norway’s national human rights institution has highlighted three main challenges. One of the recommendations is on single asylum seekers and their care services. The recommendation is to provide equivalent offers to lone minor asylum seekers and other children with child protection. The other one is about the right of seamen to sea fishing. The recommendation is to ensure that the rights of seafarers are clarified in the law so that legislation ensures that interventions which may affect the material cultural basis of seafarers are adequately investigated. The third point is a concrete proposal for a comprehensive review of whether coercive use in accordance with the Criminal Procedures Act § 222 d and the Police Act § 17 d are compatible with the Second sentence of the Basic Law. The Committee found that the best way to follow up these recommendations is to propose that the government review these recommendations and return to the Storting in an appropriate manner with regard to the follow-up of the recommendations. A unanimous committee is behind this.
ParlaMint-NO_2017-06-13.ana.ud206e7250	The majority of the population and the government are responsible for the observance of human rights in Norway. National institutions are an important – and will be, I believe, an increasingly important – contributor and pre-dispenser for how we manage our responsibilities. This is the second annual report from NIM that Stortinget is going to deal with, and we in the Center Party think it's done a good job. The institution's main tasks include: to contribute to information on human rights and to provide advice and recommendations to the responsible authorities to ensure respect for the international obligations we have. Part of the mandate for NIM is to advise the Stortingen on the human rights situation in Norway, etc. by giving this annual message to Stortinget. Of course, Norway’s national institution should benefit in addition to the annual reporting. How this is going to happen is also new for Stortinget, and the road becomes as we go. The fact that NIM is an independent institution provides some guidance for the relationship between Stortinget and NIM. Last spring, Stortinget will deal with the Annual Report from NIM for 2015. Since the organisation was newly created at that time, the annual report was not long, but it pointed out a direction for which the institution would work, and how they would do this. Last year's debate in the Stortinget expressed high expectations for NIM. It is therefore good to see that the institution has followed closely in the past year and that they come with a full annual report containing recommendations in a number of areas. Of the issues addressed by NIM in the Annual Report, several are still on the agenda of the Judicial Committee. The theme I am particularly busy with is freedom of speech and press freedom. These are among the themes mentioned in the NIM Annual Report. They show that both of these dishes are under pressure internationally, and they conclude that freedom of expression in Norway still has a strong protection, but there are also more developments in us that we would be wise to observe. We can do something about it through legislation, other things not. Because of technological developments, we're facing some problems that we haven't had before. As a result, we still have to follow and take action in line with developments in society, to ensure that human rights are respected. Like several other human rights laws, freedom of expression is not absolute. It has to be weighed against other dishes, and it has to be made subject to mandatory border crossings. For example, the importance of freedom of expression in some cases must be balanced against the concerns of privacy or other important social interests, such as social security at large. Not least, we must live with the paradox that some statements may have negative consequences for freedom of expression in themselves, such as when hateful statements cause some voices to be deterred from participating in the public debate. There must be good and thorough assessments in order to balance the concerns, and there is no easy exercise. Many more themes have been discussed in the annual NIM report, and it is not possible to address them all. What I want to say on the tape is that I'm going to include myself in the work on Stortinget. In its thematic reports, NIM has been hard hit against the authorities in some cases. It's probably better for those who are in opposition than for those who are in their position when NIM delivers criticism of decisions taken, but it's important to remember that there's just such advice to the authorities that are at the heart of the work NIM is set to do. They must have boasted that as a relatively new institution, they are clear and untrue in the fight to promote human rights. The institution has already demonstrated the ability to fulfil the role of critical counterweight to the authorities. I hope they keep doing this in the future. In a democratic society, the Biggest and changing governments themselves need to figure out how we stand up to the advice we get.

ParlaMint-NO_2017-06-13.ana.ud206e7287	It is a co-consultant's committee which has submitted adjournment following the examination of the annual report on the legal compensation scheme for 2016. The judicial compensation scheme is the Stortingen’s own scheme, where individuals may apply for discretionary compensation from the State when the person concerned has been found to have suffered a particular accident and has suffered damage or disadvantage that is not covered by general claims rules or by social security, insurance or compensation schemes. It's a system based on custom. The cases are decided by the Supreme Court Justice Committee, and it is the State Civil Court administration that has been given the task of secretariat for the selection. The Committee notes that the selection has held 10 meetings and decided 407 cases in 2016. Applications under the specific arrangements have stabilised on less than 100 cases per year. Applications by general scheme have varied, but have doctors relatively stable on about 400 cases per year in recent years. The Committee notes that 25.8 million were paid in 2016. kr in legal compensation from the State, approx. Five million. kr less than in 2014 and 2015. The Committee notes that the overall average duration of proceedings has fallen slightly, from 17 to 14 months, and welcomes it. Finally, I would like to stress that this is an important scheme. These are individual matters of importance. And that's not least part of our common history. We would therefore like to thank the Court of Justice for its work and stress the importance of all matters being dealt with as quickly as possible.

ParlaMint-NO_2017-06-13.ana.ud206e7317	When the dog law came in 2003, it was an important law. First of all, the rules of dog-holding were collected in a law, and second, humans were given a protection against dogs, in a way similar to protection against bumps. It is said that the dog is man’s best friend. We all remember "We at the Salt Crow" and "Båtsmann". But even Båtsmann was a wolf in sheep's clothing, or at least the breed Sanktbernhardshund. The Danish Animal Alliance has shown that exactly that breed is actually the race in Denmark that has the most unwanted behavior. Unwanted behavior can be so often. We know dogs can both eat and bite. What this behaviour is actually due to – therefore there is some disagreement in the professional community. Is this due to the dog's owner, or is it due to the dog and the dog's breed? At the time of the adoption of the Dog Act in 2003, a ban was imposed on certain breeds of dogs, based on the theory that some breeds were more dangerous and more aggressive than others. This claim and this assumption have really come under pressure in several European countries since 2003. Among other things, the Netherlands and Italy have lifted the ban on some dog breeds. Now it's been 14 years since the dog law was passed. There is also some new knowledge in this area. There are different perceptions of whether unwanted behaviour in dogs is due to the breed of dogs, or whether it is due to the owner. Against this background and a few other aspects of the proposal that I have not touched on, there is an overall committee that is behind the position that the Storting is dealing with today in this matter.

ParlaMint-NO_2017-06-13.ana.ud206e7402	The representative Jorodd Asphjell has taken up the suggestions he referred to.
ParlaMint-NO_2017-06-13.ana.ud206e7407	Work against violence in close relations is a priority area for the government. It's violence that goes on regardless of age, that creates insecurity and fear in the environment that would be safe. I would like to thank the proposers for putting the search light on the elderly as a vulnerable group for violence in close relations. The older part of the population is more vulnerable in abusive situations and may often be in a relationship with the perpetrator. The possibility of detachment and re-establishment is reduced by increasing age. When we also know that violence against the elderly belongs to the most hidden and most accessible form of violence in the population, there is every reason to take this seriously, and this government does. The action plan against violence in close relationships is a central instrument and contains a number of measures relevant to the elderly. Our actions in this area are general and do not relate to the age of the offended. Representatives promote three concrete proposals for strengthening efforts to combat violence against the elderly. A comprehensive study, better and more accessible information and, finally, a strengthening of the knowledge of violence against older people in municipalities and among those who meet older people in their work are requested. I agree with the proposers that knowledge of the extent, causes, character and consequences of violence is essential for targeted prevention and good quality in the relief services and in the police. No specific national scope study on violence against the elderly has been carried out in Norway, but this thematic area is part of the five-year research programme on violence in close relations funded by the Ministry of Justice and Defence. The report ‘Community action plans against violence in close relations — also for the elderly vulnerable?’ was launched in 2016. It has been examined whether municipal action plans against violence in close relations include older people at risk. To further strengthen the basis of competence in violence against the elderly, NKVTS is monitoring lifetime prevalence of violence in close relations with the population — those 66 years of age or older. Through this project, we will get several points of view on how the prevention of violence should be directed towards the older age group. On behalf of the Ministry of Health and Welfare, the Institute of Public Health, in cooperation with the Directorate of Health, has carried out an assessment of available data sources with a view to developing possible indicators of violence in close relationships. Work report will be sent to the Ministry of Health and Welfare shortly. With this as a hindrance, I find, like the majority of the committee, that there is no need for further action at this stage. With regard to the need for more targeted information for the elderly exposed to violence in close relationships, a national contact telephone has already been established for the elderly exposed to violence. The ‘Protection of the Elderly — National Contact Telephone’ project was established in 2008. Work is in progress to make the services more known to the auxiliaries. The other information measures on violence in close relationships are directed at the whole population. The police information campaign ‘How little are you going to find yourself in?’ is an example of this. The aim of the campaign is to give increased attention and knowledge of violence in close relationships, and of what the police can help with, so that the victim can seek help and get out of the violent situation. I will also bring forward the website dinutvej.no, on violence in close relations and rape, which was launched in 2016. The portal provides up-to-date and quality-assured information to vulnerable people, relatives, practitioners and auxiliaries on violence in close relations and rape. During 2017, we will also establish victim care offices in each of the 12 police districts of the country. Victim care agencies shall provide psychosocial support, follow-up, information and guidance to victims throughout the criminal proceedings. A very important target group of offices is exposed to violence in close relationships. Victim welfare offices shall be located with the police. Overall, I believe that the information measures we have already undertaken to a large extent respond to the needs of the proposers. However, I will continue to assess the need for even more targeted measures. Good knowledge is a prerequisite for providing good care. I therefore support the intentions of the proposal to strengthen the level of knowledge both in the municipality and in the first line. In the context of the implementation of the close police reform, a function such as police contact should be established, such as a link and contact point between the police and the municipality. This will be an important contribution to the dissemination of knowledge about violence against the elderly and how this can be detected and prevented. Crisis centres offer all victims of violence in close relationships, regardless of age and gender. A guide to the Crisis Centres Act has been developed, with the main target group being the municipalities, which, under the Crisis Centres Act, is entrusted with the responsibility of providing a crisis centre offer, as well as supervisors. NKVTS has compiled an overview of existing routine guides and monitoring tools in the auxiliaries for the detection of violence in close relationships. The overview provides a basis for assessing the areas in which new routine guides or guidelines are needed, and shows, among other things, the need for guidance. shortcomings related to guidance on the detection of violence against the elderly and child care failures as well as the detection of violence in the field of mental health protection against children and adults. The Directorate of Labour and Welfare has carried out a survey of how the Nav offices are involved in issues dealing with violence in close relationships. The study has been followed up by revising the supervisor on violence in close relations. In the end, I would like to mention that the Ministry of Health and Welfare has promoted a bill for the Stortinget on the responsibilities of health care services for preventing, detecting and averting violence and sexual abuse, and that this should be made clear. This will further contribute to increased awareness of and accountability for the prevention, detection and averting of violence and sexual abuse in the provision of health and care services.
ParlaMint-NO_2017-06-13.ana.ud206e7445	This proposal from the Labour Party addresses an important problem that is still taboo in many ways, namely violence against the elderly. An international review from 2015 of available studies in the field showed that assaults against the elderly are more common than we want to believe. Older people with reduced cognitive capacity, physical impairment or psychosocial distress are at increased risk of abuse. Infringements against the elderly may include mental, physical and sexual abuse, care failure and economic exploitation. According to the National Knowledge Centre on Violence and Traumatic Stress, most abuses occur against the elderly in the private sector and from those close to them. These may be spouses, children, grandchildren, relatives, auxiliary staff or others upon whom the elderly are dependent. In an institution, there may be an employee or another resident. Astrid Sandmoe, of the National Knowledge Centre on Violence and Traumatic Stress, took a doctorate in 2011 with the theme ‘Parents who are subjected to abuse by persons in their community’. She explains that the figures are internationally cracking, among other things. because different methods of data collection are used, and there are different perceptions of the nature of violence and abuse against the elderly. But the studies from England and Ireland are solid, and they can provide us with a basis for comparison. In the elderly in Norway, it is estimated that between 2 and 3 pst. experiences violence in close relationships. If you expand your perspective and think that close relationships also include friends, neighbors, or others the older person has a close relationship to, the number is raised to about 4 pst. by the population. In Norway, as the Minister of Justice also said, we do not have very good occurrence studies. We have some smaller studies and living conditions studies, but they catch up to a small extent with the older part of the population. Astrid Sandmoe stresses that men too are subjected to violence, as we all know, but men are more often affected by violence outside the home, while women are more often hit in the home. The Labour Party has another draft representative for treatment in Stortinget, Document 8:89 S for 2016–2017, which calls for an action plan for the mental health of the elderly, precisely with proposals to strengthen efforts in the primary health service for the prevention, diagnosis and treatment of mental disorders in the elderly. A municipal action plan in this field will also reveal whether an older person is or has been subjected to violence. Through several good measures that can lie in such a plan, we will be able to catch up with the elderly who do not dare, or have the courage, to tell about their own experiences of violence, such as violence. is committed by a related person. Such an action plan as this is somewhat more concrete and clearly directed towards older people than information. Then we can discover more and also find out more about some of the mental problems and anxiety of the elderly due to just violence. But unfortunately, it doesn't look like the government parties are doing well to support the good proposal.

ParlaMint-NO_2017-06-13.ana.ud206e7516	I am pleased with the great commitment to the framework conditions of the courts, and I agree that the procedure in the courts should not be too long. The project was launched this year, with a budget of 28 million. kr, and shall be scheduled to take place for more than six years and initially include the twelve largest courts of procedure, the courts of law and the Supreme Court. There are several good reasons why the project should be implemented in stages. The transition from paper-based to full-digital proceedings during and after the main proceedings will require much from the Court of Justice and the individual courts. Starting digital courts in the twelve largest district courts, lawyers’ courts and Supreme Courts only reduces the risk during the implementation phase. It reduces the likelihood of delays, budget exceedances and errors in the digital solutions. Such errors may, in the ultimate sense, lead to a longer period of proceedings, and may compromise the quality of the proceedings conducted by the courts. The implementation risk must also be seen in the context of several other major projects in progress in the courts and in the Court of Justice. Since 2014, the Court’s procedure has been further developed and all procedural systems have been given the same platform, Lovisa. The Land Change Courts are in the process of implementing a new procedural system in 2017 and a new one is under development for the Supreme Court. Furthermore, the electronic interaction between the courts, police and criminal services shall be improved through the project ESAS, formerly known as Stifliner II. The judicial administration is planning the project together with the Directorate of Police and the Directorate of Criminal Affairs. In addition, the Court of Justice, on the basis of a separate trial project in Tromsø, shall draw up a plan for the introduction of sound and video recording in the courts throughout the country. The scope of digital courts is also a question of admissibility and a priority between many important initiatives in the field of justice. Therefore, the government decided to start the project where the gains are greater. The twelve largest courts of jurisdiction, lawyers' courts and Supreme Courts account for about half of the total number of cases in the general courts, and they deal with the majority of the largest criminal cases. At the same time, I would like to draw attention to the fact that parts of the functionality of the Digital Courts will be made available to all courts in the first stage. In the long run, it's natural that all the courts become full-digital. The benefits of reducing the length of proceedings in the main courts will also increase the overall scope of action of the courts. According to the Court of Justice estimates, the gains will start to rise as early as 2018 and gradually to 2023. During 2017, the Ministry of Justice and Security will reduce a sample such as: shall determine the future structure of the courts. It is likely that the sample may propose amendments which may affect the conduct of digital courts in the remaining Courts of First Instance. I also believe this is a good reason to implement digital courts in several stages. So far, the Ministry has had a good dialogue with the Court of Justice on the implementation of the project. We will maintain this dialogue, so that we can put a plan for the roll-out of the project in all courts with a low implementation risk, and take into account any changes in the court structure.

So in my opinion, there are many issues that need to be improved in the next enlargement period, but now we are dealing with a measure that can improve the situation of employees as it is today – something that the employees themselves have pointed to as a necessary solution. For the Labour Party, it is important that policies in Norway facilitate and stimulate growth in the number of jobs, so that people are given security for a job to go to. It is equally important for the Workers' Party that when you have a job, the employees should be safe in the job they have. Criminal staff have for a long time pointed to insecurity challenges. We know that the level of threats and violence has increased dramatically in criminal matters under this government. This development should trigger a great deal of political commitment. Nevertheless, today's government has not engaged itself worth mentioning before March/April 2017, and I have to say that the new Minister of Justice seems more responsive than his predecessor. That's good. It is also good that the Directorate of Criminal Welfare took seriously the concerns of the employees and started work on establishing a service number scheme with the employees’ organisations. That being said, it is gratifying that the majority of the Grand Duchy has moved in the direction of the Labour Party’s view, namely that the top political body in Norway — the Stortinget — should be involved in criminal matters. It is also gratifying that the Ministry, in its letter to the Committee, expresses its intention to follow up the matter when the work of the sample is completed. The Committee agrees to ask the Stortinge to bring the matter back in the autumn of 2017. Then a new accumulation can assess the results of the work and then decide on the matter.
ParlaMint-NO_2017-06-13.ana.ud206e7686	What the proposers point out is an important principle of legal certainty – that the disclosure law and the data protection law also apply to prisoners in prison. Prisoners in Norwegian prisons have the right of access to documents dealing with them themselves, including assessments made for criminal offences committed for offences against legislation, refusals to manufacture and leave, etc. The representative proposal indicates that there has been a development of violence and threats in Norwegian prisons from 2011 to 2016 and that the introduction of employee service numbers will provide them with better protection. It is true that there has been a strong increase in registered violence and threats in the criminal justice system from 2014 to 2016. I share the concern for an increased number of violent and threatening episodes. At the same time, it is positive that it is working to raise registration awareness, so that several events are also recorded. The Ministry closely monitors developments in violence and threats, including: in the governance dialogue with the Directorate of Criminal Welfare. It is important to put in place proper measures to reduce violence and threats to employees. It is also important that employees remain an active co-player, together with the prisoners, creating a natural and positive environment and good interaction together. Spending several years in prison and using only one number in the dialogue with employees could be alienating and contributing to the opposite of what you want to achieve. Prison inmates are to be rehabilitated, and the prerequisite for this is also good relations between the officers and the prisoners. Open dialogue provides a basis for better climate. This can contribute to less violence and threats than if the prisoners did not become acquainted with the employees. This will also ensure greater security in the job. Also, a service number alone will not eliminate all opportunities for violence and threats as long as it includes: is the right of access to work logs where events are recorded with the employee’s name, and there is a view from prison to the parking places where employees’ cars are registered with their name and address. Therefore, as the Committee points out, other measures need to be considered. After dialogue with employees’ organisations, the Directorate of Criminal Welfare will examine the possibilities for better protection of employees in various service activities. A working group has been set up to address the issue. The working group will assess whether service numbers or similar traceable anonymous identity should be used in some types of situations and events. The working group aims to present a proposal during this autumn. I am concerned about the safety of employees and understand their concerns. I would therefore like to follow up this issue and consider further actions when the Directorate of Criminal Welfare has submitted its assessment after the working group's investigation, and I will return to the matter in an appropriate manner.

ParlaMint-NO_2017-06-13.ana.ud206e7719	This issue concerns the establishment of a European Agency for the operational management of large-scale IT systems in the area of freedom, security and justice, and the conclusion of the ancillary agreement. The establishment of the Agency is an improvement in the operation of the systems, and the development of new large IT systems is also for the benefit of Norway. Through the additional agreement negotiated, Norway is given a limited vote on the Management Board of the Agency and Norway’s financial contribution will follow the general formula for the calculation of Norway’s financial contribution to Schengen cooperation and Eurodac. The regulation does not require any legislative or regulatory changes, but participation in the Agency requires decision-making and the consent of the Storting to the acceptance of the act, and the supplementary agreement is necessary in accordance with the second paragraph of Section 26 of the Constitution. A unanimous committee, except for the Central Party, is behind the vote, and I assume that the Central Party will explain its views on the matter.
ParlaMint-NO_2017-06-13.ana.ud206e7730	I'll be refreshing cards. The previous association agreements Norway has with the EU do not cover the activities of new EU bodies that are being developed within the framework of Schengen and Eurodac-related actions. Therefore, an additional EU-Noreg agreement was negotiated in April 2016 to regulate the participation of the Agency. This includes other participation in the Management Board and advisory committees, the financial contribution and staff management from Norway. The reason why the Central Party does not vote for this proposition is that there has been no clarification from the Court of Justice of the European Union on the rules on voting rights at all in line with EU law. I do not think it is right that we should at this point give authority to renegotiate the agreement, precisely because we do not know whether he is in breach of EU law. The proposition states that it is only if a renegotiated agreement requires a promise decision that the matter will be referred back to Stortinget. In reality, we do not know what we are voting for today, and the Central Party therefore believes that the government must wait and re-examine the matter after the European Court of Justice has settled the matter. It is worth noting that the European Commission will not sign the agreement until the European Court of Justice has delivered its opinion. I include this in the report from the Centre Party.

ParlaMint-NO_2017-06-13.ana.ud206e7748	Europe has again felt, through new reprehensible and cowardly terrorist attacks in the United Kingdom, that some environments do not stand back in terms of killing, harm and grief. No nation alone is able to combat radical Islam and the seemingly increasing will to commit acts of terrorism. Preventing acts of terrorism requires, on the contrary, close international cooperation at several levels and across several sectors. In the EU, this is among the highest priority areas, and it was at the latest in the last week’s Justice and Home Affairs Council. I would like to point out that through our participation and good cooperation with the European Union in the field of justice and home affairs, the government is helping to provide Norwegian policies with a safer everyday life for citizens. Both in order to prevent and to combat it, we need to ensure that the agencies that need it have access to the necessary information in a timely manner across borders. Relevant information is now widely disseminated through electronic systems. In order to professionalise both the development of new large IT systems and the operational management of existing systems, the EU set up in 2011 a dedicated agency for these purposes, the so-called eu-LISA. The current central IT systems in the field of justice and home affairs are therefore now managed by eu-LISA. It concerns the Schengen Information System, the SIS, the Visa Information System, the VIS, and Eurodac, the fingerprint comparison database for asylum applications. The EU's objective in this field is to create a comprehensive and strategic view of IT development in the field of justice and home affairs. New IT systems are under discussion and under development. These will be subsequently added to the Agency. Through its operational management and development of new IT systems, the Agency supports EU policies on asylum, migration and border control. We are talking here about key elements of the work on internal security and security in the EU/Schengen area. In order for Norway to participate in the Management Board of the Agency and thus participate in strategic developments, it had to negotiate a separate supplementary agreement with the EU. While the establishment of eu-LISA is a proper further development of the Schengen and Eurodac acquis, new EU bodies set up within them are not covered by our association agreement. The agreement on Norwegian participation in eu-LISA was finalised in April 2016. A number of the conditions regulated by the agreement are referred to in previous agreements on Norwegian participation in other EU agencies. The main issue this time was the extent to which Norway and the other Schengen-related countries could vote on the Agency’s Management Board and Advisory Group. The EU has said that they wish to submit the agreement to the Court of Justice of the European Union once all Schengen-related countries have notified the regulation. The European Court of Justice will then be asked to assess whether the provision on voting rights is compatible with EU law. If the Court finds that the rules are contrary to EU law, the agreement will have to be renegotiated. Otherwise, the contract may be signed and enter into force. Should a renegotiated agreement require a decision to grant that deviates significantly from the draft presented in the proposal, the matter will of course be referred back to the Stortinget.

ParlaMint-NO_2017-06-13.ana.ud206e7949	What are we going to do with the humanities? This question is the title of a report on the humanities situation in Norway published by Fritt Ord in 2014. The report is written by a working group chaired by Tore Rem and Helge Jordheim. There is no official investigation, but it asks questions like: What are we going to do with literature, and art, and history and language? The book discusses the situation of the humanities of the Norwegian community and includes proposals for possible course changes and measures to strengthen Norwegian humanities in response to current and future challenges. Because as a society, there's no doubt that we actually need humanistic knowledge. The message we are discussing today, Humaniora in Norway, is an answer to how the government perceives that the societal potential of the humanities can be fully triggered in response to today's major questions, in the food and cultural life, in school, and for example. also in a central community institution such as the Defence. This is the first political document to provide a comprehensive review of the humanities field in Norway. But the message goes into a wide range of ambitious government issues about higher education and research. To the right, it is a prerequisite for meeting the complex challenges of society that we build new knowledge and ability to connect existing knowledge from different disciplines. As rapporteur, I would like to take this opportunity to thank the Committee for its good cooperation during the debate. The Committee notes that the Communication describes the reality and challenges of the field in a good way, and that the humanists have too little knowledge and skills to use in tackling the major societal challenges. The purpose of the communication is precisely to facilitate a better release of the potential of humanities in society and in the workplace and in schools. In the Communication, the government is bringing forward the climate, demographic and technological changes we are facing, such as areas where the needs for more and better humane knowledge and competences are significant, and problems affecting how the professions can contribute to meeting these challenges. An important step in the Communication is to facilitate the strengthening of humanities in the major research programmes, while taking seriously the relevance of the humanities disciplines when assessed at their own premises. Other objectives of the Communication are enhanced academic quality in humanistic research, better opportunities for humanities candidates in the labour market and a better link between academic humanities and the school. The priorities and measures put forward by the government for humanistic research and education in this Communication are in line with the long-term priorities of the Long-term Programme for Research and Higher Education and the objectives of the Quality Communication for Higher Education, which we discussed last week. The long-term plan contains both general priorities for quality and more thematic priorities for meeting the major challenges of our time. This is also the approach of the humanities communication, where the government first discusses the general framework for improving quality and then the need for humanities in thematic areas. A society is always moving, and therefore political changes and adjustments are also necessary. However, there is also a need for changes both at the humanistic research and education institutions and in the social and labour life that are to use humanistic knowledge disseminated through research results and candidates. In Norway, since World War II, we have had clear political expectations and demands for subjects such as science, technology, engineering and mathematics. Large public investment has been made in laboratories and equipment. Social sciences have also been the subject of great political interest. But we have not had similar political expectations or demands for humanities. Now we get that. The Communication sets out measures to ensure that the policy framework we have is more appropriate to the specific nature of humanities. It makes it visible that not only in universities and colleges important humanistic research is carried out, but also in archives, libraries, museums and research institutes. Finally, I will give a good example of what we might see more of in the future. This morning I visited the Olympic peak, where I met the top sports manager in the Norwegian Sports Federation, Tore Oberebø. He's a trained philosopher, he's a former top sportsman himself, and he's using his skills to the full in the top management job, where he's going to help build up Norwegian athletes at the top international level to pick up the most awarded medals in international championships. It is a recognition that the humanists have knowledge and skills and tools that are too little used to deal with major societal challenges – not for humanists to change their peculiarity, but because they are in perspective we are totally dependent on. If we look around today's world at climate change, migration and rapid technology shifts, we are quick to deal with issues like identity, values, religion, culture and ethics, i.e. humanistic perspectives. Many of these issues come out of the way people behave. To understand their motives, we also need to know something about the world's ideas. In short, the humanities are vital if we are to navigate well through some of the changes we are facing as a society. This Communication responds well to the challenges, and in the roll-out of the long-term plan, coming to the harvest, these perspectives will be seen in the context.

ParlaMint-NO_2017-06-13.ana.ud206e8215	Today we are dealing with a big message about learning, early work and quality in the school. I want to start by thanking the Committee for a good cooperation. The government points out in the Communication that the Norwegian school is in a positive development, but that there are also challenges. Central measures in the Communication are to introduce a legal obligation to early action, to enhance the competence of specialised education in schools and to introduce a lower school-quality threshold, where schools that score below the lower limit are to be assisted by a coaching body. In the long term, the government will consider requiring municipalities to receive assistance from such guidance corps. Increased support is also proposed for local work on quality development and an innovation fund for testing actions and local school research projects. The batches agree on a lot in the setting, but there is also a disagreement about some points. I'm assuming that the different parties will explain their positions, and now I'm going to explain the Christian People's Party's view of this message. I believe that the message contains good intentions, and also many good suggestions. It is a good idea to clarify the obligation to provide intensive training to students who are dependent on reading, writing and billing. I am pleased that an overall committee supports this being a low-threshold offer without a requirement for extensive proceedings. Anyone who has problems cracking the reading code does not always have time to wait for investigations and proceedings. Help must come right away. Nevertheless, it is important that this does not go beyond the right to expert judgement and individual decisions on special education. But the most important thing that needs to be put in place for the students to get the help they need as quickly as possible is teachers who have enough time to help them. That's why we have to stop crowded classrooms and teachers who have to go home after the end of working day with a bad conscience because they didn't get to help everyone who needed help. That's why we need more teachers, and the Christian People's Party has had an important impact in this period: 1.3 billion. kr in earmarked funds for several teachers from 1 to 4. class. All Norwegian municipalities have now been given the opportunity to recruit more teachers for the youngest students. But we also need a norm, clear rules that say something about how many students we can have per teacher at each school, and we need a norm at school level and for the whole primary school, as the Christian People's Party has suggested twice in this big time period. When we want to give teachers more time for each child, it's also important that we manage to recruit enough teachers. I will perhaps come back to that in a later article in the debate. It is also important that those in need of special education receive it, and it is important that they are provided with the help of a educator who has the competence to help them. It's a bad idea to give those students who need the most help to get help from the least qualified teachers, but there's unfortunately the situation in many places today. The government will consider introducing a further training offer in special pedagogics for teachers. That is good, but the majority of the committee has already carried out an assessment and stated in its opinion that this should simply be done. Then I think it is great with an offer of state aid to those municipalities and schools that are struggling a little extra to get a proper swing on the learning process. We have to set quality standards for all schools, but we know that there are great variations in how much school actually contributes to students' learning. Nevertheless, I think it is wrong to identify which schools need drug treatment by introducing a quantitatively defined lower limit after which all schools should be assessed. Quantitative information must not be the only measure of quality. I think we can manage to help those schools that need help, and figure out which schools it is, without introducing another national ranking of schools with all the negative consequences it will bring. I think the schools that are struggling know that for themselves. I think the municipalities that are struggling know for themselves. I think that the State, with its academic competence, also knows that, and I think it needs a more discretionary assessment, based on a broader set of indicators. I therefore hope that the proposal of the Workers' Party, the Christian People's Party and the Left to introduce a follow-up scheme based on a broader set of indicators reflecting the school's broad societal tasks, as well as the County's discretionary judgements, will have a majority. Then I'll bring up those suggestions in the setting that the Christian People's Party is part of.

ParlaMint-NO_2017-06-13.ana.ud206e8252	Every single day, thousands of planes are flying and landing in Norway. A comprehensive apparatus is in turn to get us from A to B: the personnel taking care of the check-in, security controls, the total flight review before each take-off, cabin crew, pilots and employees in the flight tower. A sea of technical factors must be in place to do well, as must a sea of human factors. We simply do not accept that it should go wrong. All aeroplanes shall proceed safely. Every day tens of thousands of lessons are offered in the Norwegian school for even more students. A comprehensive set of tools has been put in place for students to learn as much as possible: skilled teachers, educational support staff, child and youth workers, janitors, SFO staff, etc. A sea of human, material and technical factors must be in place to do well. In spite of this, we accept that a little bit too many people don't get completely into the goal. Every year there are 28 000 students who do not complete basic training at a normal time. Around half complete and never complete the initial training. Many of these have already fallen from primary school. This is a matter of urgency. This was the introduction to the post I held in this room in 2015, and it really fits just as well now. The reason for this was the Workers' Party's proposal to increase early efforts, which included: suggested that all students who needed it and who followed it should be quickly provided with additional training so that they learned to read, write and calculate within the second stage. The parties to the Government voted against the proposal. So 2015 went over to 2016, and 2016 went over to 2017, and now, on the very last day of the debate, the Education Committee has in Stortinget, we are going to discuss a big message where we recognize much of the same thing that we proposed in 2015. Let me first say that we're happy that we're now going a step further. But this could have happened a lot before. The reasons have been many. The government has voted against several good proposals – yes, five or more times they have voted against our proposals to increase early effort in this period. The year has passed, even more have fallen away, and that is not good. We agree that there is a need for a reinforced obligation to follow-up of students who are following, but if it is to work optimally, we also need more teachers who can spend more time helping each student to achieve their goals. This is one of the reasons why the Workers' Party wants more teachers in school and that a national standard for teacher density should be established. We want more teachers, especially the youngest students, because it helps to ensure early work. Paragraphs are not enough alone; there is also a need for more wise, caring and helpful heads. One of the things that has created the most debate in the Communication is the way the government will design a lower limit of school quality on which the schools are to be measured. Many hearing bodies have been critical of this. We fully agree with the Student Organisation that there is a need to create a system that ensures more that students receive an equivalent training offer across the country, but that the set of indicators to be considered by municipalities must be broader than the government has proposed. It does not include a tab of the student survey and any selected results on national tests; the training of students is too important to create such a narrow assessment system. That is why, together with the Christian People's Party and the Left, we are promoting an alternative proposal to create a follow-up system in which additional assistance and guidance are offered to municipalities and schools based on a broader and more comprehensive set of indicators, as well as the County's discretionary assessments. In this way, we get a follow-up scheme where the focus is more knowledge and more insight, so that the schools and municipalities that need it can get even better help. The working party considers that it is also appropriate to consider whether to allow the State to provide guidance to municipalities and schools that do not provide a training offer of satisfactory quality over time. Time is running out, and my speaking time is almost over. Five minutes goes very fast. It also does four years, but there'll be limits, the government has spent too long on this. This can't be called early effort, on the contrary, this is late effort. I bring up the proposals that the Workers' Party is behind in the document that has not already been included.

ParlaMint-NO_2017-06-13.ana.ud206e8274	In fact, Norwegian school has been on a rather spectacular journey since 2001. We're the country that spends the most money per student, but we don't have the best results in the world. This means that more resources do not automatically lift the results. We also need to discuss what we're spending resources on, how we're fixing resources, and how we're helping those who need it the most. In 2001, when there was a change of government, the previous bourgeois government started work on the Knowledge promise, as well as introducing national tests, transparency of results and an open societal debate on what is good at school, and what can be better. We have already received some of the results of the work that was done that time. For example, the international PIRLS survey, which measures students’ literacy skills, shows that Norway, after the introduction of the Knowledge Promise, has gone from 12 pst. by our fourth grade cut below the critical limit in reading, that now there is only 1 pst. which cuts below the critical limit. In many ways it can be said that when the Knowledge promise came, it was one of the most important not just school reforms, but the social policy reforms that have been made in this millennium. And now, in this period, we have had a new bourgeois government that has carried out the teacher's vow of time – a teacher's vow that has once again recognised that we are using most in the world, but that we do not have the best results in the world. So we have to spend the money on what creates more learning. And by strengthening the most important input for students' learning, the teacher in the classroom, they have now also raised their skills and ability in Norwegian school to adapt their teaching better. School policy takes time. From something introduced to it shows changes at the other end, it takes time. Now the bourgeois government is proposing a new, important reform, namely a large-scale reform of early school work. The fact that you are now saying that you are not just going through a simple decision, but actually have a right to adapt education and to receive special education if you need it is in many ways a revolution for those students who have experienced that they have been sitting on the school bench either and bored themselves because they have learned too quickly, or that they have fallen off because the teaching has gone too fast. At the same time, they say we should have a closer follow-up of the municipalities as school owners. Yes, it is good that we have local school owners, yes, it is good that the municipalities and the counties are the ones responsible for the schools in Norway, but it cannot be that where Mother and Dad bought houses, the chances of success at school are determined. Professor of pedagogy at the Hedmark High School, Thomas Nordahl, has been able to demonstrate that if you are a boy who lives in North Hedmark and has two parents with only high school, you have three times as much chance of completing and passing high school as if you are a boy and living in South Hedmark. The reason is that in Nord-Hedmark, six municipalities have joined forces to do quality work, measure quality, but also discuss how they can lift students. That's very good. But it cannot be left to chance where that effort is put. That is why it is so important that we now have a message that seems to have a great deal of support, which says that we should set a lower limit on school quality and put measures in place and offer help to the municipalities that need it. And it's not just about knowledge or the ability to read or write or really calculate, which is incredibly important, it's also about students' well-being and that too many students get bullied at school and experience nothing happening. That's why it's also a criterion to measure school quality that the students are well-being and well-being. In addition, the ability and willingness of local politicians, municipalities and counties to be active school owners must be preserved. This is why school skills are also strengthened, so that teachers, school leaders and municipal politicians together can drive more school development than they do today and have the skills to do just that. It's also about strengthening the skills of the principals, so that they can be what they really are: an executive in a workplace where you not only have to produce values, like in other companies, but actually produce whole people who will succeed and live the rest of their lives. So this message is essentially, not to say the whole of it, being accepted by the Stortingen today. It deserves it, because the Council of State has done a thorough and good job of taking the next step in the bourgeois school reform: from the promise of the Knowledge, to the Teacher's promise, to a grand boost of early effort.
ParlaMint-NO_2017-06-13.ana.ud206e8291	‘Teaching — Early Work and Quality in School’ is a very important message, and I am pleased that the government has presented it. It contains a lot of good measures, which will provide students with better learning outcomes in school. Important measures in the Communication are to introduce a legal obligation to early action, to enhance the competence of specialised education in schools, to make higher demands on the proportion of pedagogues in kindergarten and to strengthen the competence of school leaders. Among the measures the Progress Party will bring forward is the proposal to introduce a lower school-quality threshold. The municipalities that may be located below the lower limit will, among other things: be calculated from the results of national reading and billing tests, primary school scores, learning environment — and the proportion of students who declare that they are being bullied or kept out. In our view, this is a broad range of criteria, which will provide a good and varied picture of students' learning outcomes at school. The current municipalities will receive assistance from a school-experienced guidance team. There is a lot of good happening in Norwegian school, but there is no doubt that there are also basic challenges in school, which have to be met through, among other things. a stronger commitment to early action and strengthening the skills of teachers. The introduction of master’s education, enhanced further training and the introduction of a test scheme of teacher specialists are key steps that the government has put in place and can have a significant impact on the further development of the role of teachers in the future. For the Progress Party, it has always been a goal for students to learn more at school. Then it is essential to capture and support students who need help. Research shows that early in the course of training measures have a higher impact on pupils’ learning and personal development than measures later in the course of training. It is also important to increase the proportion of students who complete and pass secondary education. The progress point here will indicate that the implementation rate has increased to 73 pst. in recent years, which is the highest degree of implementation since the start of the measurement. Hopefully that suggests that we're on the right track. When the SSB brought this up, they said that there is reason to believe that the concrete quality measures that have been implemented have had a clear impact. Increased emphasis on early intervention, national real-life investment and quality enhancement in teacher training – in combination with higher skills requirements – are important factors. In addition, the Professional League has included a number of measures – which the Progress Party has worked for for a number of years – to reduce the drop-out rate. The Progress Party is pleased that there has been close dialogue between teacher training environments, KS and organisations working on a strategy for teacher training. It is an objective to establish mutually committed and developing partnerships between teacher training and schools and kindergartens. In this context, we in the Progress Party will point out the importance of the school’s full use of the competence of the teacher’s staff. The planning and implementation of the teaching and evaluation of and feedback on student work must be the primary task of the teacher. The school must therefore have a different apparatus to deal with reporting and commercial tasks. The teacher's main task is to be a teacher, not a bureaucrat. There are still some challenges in this area. The Progress Party will also highlight the important role of the school libraries as a tool to support students in achieving the objectives set out in the curriculum. International research shows that children and young people who read varied scientific literature at the school library and in classrooms develop an commitment to reading. The studies also show that well-developed school libraries and partnerships between teachers and librarians are essential for the development of a reading culture. School libraries are also important in learning source criticism. The Progress Party would point out that the Training Act is clear that students should have access to the school library throughout their initial training. Finally, I would like to stress that there is a lot of good work done in the school. Unfortunately, it is also such that many students are met with a waiting-and-see attitude, instead of taking hold and giving students who are struggling, early efforts. Therefore, the Progress Party is pleased that schools are required to make early efforts. This, together with the various measures taken and the measures contained in the communication, will improve pupils’ learning outcomes in school.

We believe that there is reason for the ministry to pay extra attention to this and consider whether the exemption from masters to be admitted to PPU training needs to be extended for a longer period. Such an assessment considers that we need to be consulted with the sector. The left is concerned that all kids should go to a good school. Most municipalities and county councils are good school owners who work for the best of their students, but we agree with the government that there is still a need to follow up the municipalities that over time are unable to keep a good enough level. In order for this not only to be a ranking in the newspapers of which schools are poor, and which are good, we believe it is important to look a little more comprehensively at what should be assessed when we talk about quality. There should also be some more discretionary exercise related to it, and we believe that it can also make the follow-up more targeted. The comments mention proposals for criteria that can be included, but I would like to stress the importance of a professional assessment of what is appropriate. We cannot risk the indicators leading to excessive numbers of schools being followed up. This should be an offer to those who labor the most.
ParlaMint-NO_2017-06-13.ana.ud206e8366	The debate on the future school has been increasingly marked in recent years by new directions and new tones, but it is largely due to the government. This is due not least to the recommendations made by the expert committee chaired by Sten Ludvigsen – the so-called Ludvigsen Committee – that the school needs to improve its knowledge, that the curricula must have fewer objectives, and that there must be more time and space for in-depth learning, creativity and lifelong learning in Norwegian schools. Despite the fact that the Storting largely agreed with the advice of the Ludvigsen Committee on what the school of the future should look like, the government has little followed it up in this communication. On the contrary, I believe that the message and government in crucial areas are taking the school in the wrong direction. With some of the proposals in this Communication, the government reinforces target management based on control and ranking, leading to further narrowing of school knowledge and building on further theorisation and uniformisation of education, as revealed by the evaluation of the Knowledge promise. A child does not prepare for the future by mastering as many learned responses as possible in the shortest possible time. Instead, more depth learning is needed, so that students can understand the core of the subjects, see contexts across subjects, and learn how to find and make use of new knowledge. In the future school, practical and active learning needs to be used to a greater extent than today, and there must be room for creativity and culture. Therefore, the most important thing for the future school is: more teachers, so there will be more time for each individual student a school trust reform to reduce unnecessary target management, control, ranking and testing; time and space in school for a more practical and varied school day, which is why SV proposes a time shift in primary school where we will take time from the school leisure system and home lessons and create a longer day in primary school – the full day school – which can provide the basis for a more practical and varied school day Increasing teacher density will enable schools to succeed in making early efforts. It will give teachers a better opportunity to create a more practical and varied day of school for everyone. SV has on several occasions made proposals to amend the national quality assessment system. It is a central part of our proposal for a trust reform in school, and it is in line with the recommendations of the Ludvigsen Committee. We will therefore once again ask the government, in cooperation with the sector, to do something about the quality assessment system. In this communication, however, the government is proposing a follow-up scheme in which municipalities with poor school results over time are offered assistance from the guidance body. We are strongly in favour of assistance from the guidance body, but in order to identify the municipalities that need assistance, the ministry has moved in to develop a so-called indicator set that defines a lower limit of quality. There is strong concern in the sector about what this will mean, that too much emphasis on test results, user surveys and other quantifiable results will be another contribution to narrowing down the school’s broad societal remit and not least too easily understanding quality. Again we see what has become a characteristic of this government in the field of knowledge: it does not listen to professionals, and it does not listen to teachers in particular. We believe that the County and education authorities have the information that is needed to identify municipalities and schools that are struggling. Therefore, we do not believe that there is a need for a so-called national lower limit based on a narrow indicator set. As it appears that the majority will nevertheless support the establishment of such a lower limit, the SV will vote in favour of the proposal of the Workers' Party, the Christian People's Party and the Left that in this case it will have to be based on a broader set of indicators. Indicators for both structure, process and performance quality should be included in the case. We do that in the hope that with it we can contribute to the majority for this. I will return to a couple of other points that are important for SV in connection with the message.
ParlaMint-NO_2017-06-13.ana.ud206e8397	The purpose paragraph to which the Stortinget joined a few years ago, sets some very ambitious goals for the Norwegian school. The school will open up the future and the world to the students. Students shall develop knowledge and skills that enable them to master their lives and to participate in working and social life. The school shall meet the students with confidence, respect and demands and provide them with challenges that promote education and learning. Reaching those goals is not the responsibility of teachers, principals or others who work at school alone. It is also our responsibility – as elected representatives. We are the ones who have the opportunity to make these visions reality. Equally important is the fact that we are aware that Norwegian school should also not be controlled in detail by flooding or government in order to achieve these objectives. Our job as politicians is to help the school solve the problem of giving all children and young people good learning, more knowledge and a good school day. This is precisely what the apprenticeship message is about — facilitating quality development in schools adapted to local needs. At the same time, we also make clear demands for the student because it must always weigh heavily – regardless of demographics, geography or economics. We can't have a situation where it's so dependent on where you live, what quality and learning you get in school. I want to start with something that is surprisingly little mentioned in the setting, namely a large grip that changes the way national authorities contribute to quality improvement in school. In this work, we build on both the promise of knowledge from the Bondevik II government, Kristin Clamet and Right, and the Teacher promise from this period. With the Knowledge promise, more responsibility was transferred from the state to the municipalities. The government's major investment in, among others: further training of teachers and conditions of immersion provide a good basis for municipalities to discharge their responsibilities towards pupils. Now we're taking the next natural step, that's to use more of the means that the government uses to develop the school, through the municipalities. We want the school development measures to take place where they are actually used. The framework may be laid by the State, but it is the local analysis of what is needed to determine what action is to be taken. In 2017, the municipalities will thus receive a transfer of 100 million. kr to be used to develop the quality locally. In the long term, the state will support local school development up to half a billion per year. In addition, we are creating a system that gives us more research-based knowledge of what actually works in school, so that you don't listen to politicians who turn research results into each other. This gives us a lot of opportunities. At the same time, we also put in a safety valve, when we say that we want closer follow-up of municipalities that, for various reasons, need support and guidance in order to be able to operate their schools better. This major conversion has received a lot of support. At the same time, many people also say that it's going to take time to build good cooperation relations locally, and we have to take that into account. This message also marks a continuation and an enhanced early effort. 10,000 students go out every year with so weak skills in mathematics and reading that they can get problems with further education and work. The problem isn't always legal, but it's still so that for many places there's a wait-and-see attitude. There are too many students who are struggling professionally and socially, and who are not getting help early enough. That's why we need financial incentives. We need changes in laws, as the government suggests, and we also need a new teacher training with several initial experts sent out to school. I also want to say something about the quality assessment system. I don't understand why anyone wants to present it as a target and result management system. Training at school is characterised in Norway by a wide range of knowledge. It's a great professional room for action. The quality assessment system is there primarily to be a tool for the professional communities. What we've done is we've put this system forward in its full width, and that's because the Storting should be able to make some clear choices, not just make vague orders to the government, but if you want something to go away, be honest and say what it is you want to go away. Someone's clear. The Socialist Left Party wants to abolish national tests for everyone, the Central Party wants to get us out of the major international school surveys, but there is no majority for that. In terms of indicators, I would first like to say that these are not just narrow indicators that measure on the basis of some national samples. I would also like to discuss whether there are other indicators that better express the overall quality of the school, but there are some things we have to think about. First, there should be indicators that are the same for everyone, that is to some degree standardised. This means that, for example: The character sections can be a little bit difficult if you're going to go down on the individual subject, even though we're also using primary school credits. The second is that there should be output and not input factors, because it is not that we want to end up giving extra state support to Oslo, which may use less per pupil, but also has quite good results.

ParlaMint-NO_2017-06-13.ana.ud206e8709	The document makes the following proposals: ‘The government is asked to present its own report on a strengthened culture school for the future.’ There has been a good dialogue in the Committee on this matter. The Committee points out that cultural schools, like the public libraries, are part of the nationwide cultural infrastructure. Culture schools are an extension of the general training provision of primary school in practical-esthetic subjects and play an important role in strengthening the cultural competence and joy of children and young people. The Committee acknowledges the role of cultural schools as a contribution to the achievement of life for pupils and the community, and therefore considers it important to facilitate the continued contribution of cultural schools to the development of valuable cultural ballast for students. A majority of the Committee would also stress that it is an important principle that municipalities as owners of the cultural school should be able to determine the content and scope of the offer to a large extent. In this way, there is room for local adjustments for the many and sometimes different municipalities we have in the country. It has now been 20 years since the law enforcement of the cultural school, and there may therefore be a need to look at the development, status and achievement of the cultural school. Furthermore, it would be natural to look at the subject areas that exist and to provide better statistics on what is going on in the country's cultural schools. The Committee would stress the importance of seeing the relationship between cultural schools and schools, but it is also important to see the cultural school in the context of other cultural work. The Committee points out that civil society – the voluntary cultural and organisational life – has played a positive and important role throughout the history of modern Norway. The Committee will highlight the importance of civil society for personal responsibility, voluntary community, creativity and spirit of virtue. These are values that are important to take care of and that are crucial to the trust of society. The Committee would also point out that artistic freedom and diversity are one of the cornerstones of any democracy. It is therefore appropriate to facilitate increased cooperation with private voluntary forces in the cultural school. The Committee points out that the Ministry of Culture has recently launched work on a new communication on culture. The content of the Communication has not yet been clarified, but the Committee considers it natural that cultural schools should also be mentioned in this Communication. The majority of the Committee of the Workers' Party, the Christian People's Party, the Centre Party, the Left and the SV are aware that the Ministry of Culture has initiated the preparation of a major communication on culture, but will stress that the municipal cultural schools are an independent, statutory school team, and that it is the Minister of Knowledge who has the constitutional responsibility for the cultural schools. This majority further indicates that the cultural school has its legal foundation in the Education Act, but that no further national leadership for the school has been provided through regulations. The initiative to establish a framework for the municipal cultural schools, to decide on the future organisation of the cultural school sector and to provide national leadership for the further development of the school type is of the opinion that the Minister of Knowledge should come from the Minister of Culture rather than from the Minister of Culture. The majority of the Committee therefore considers that the Ministry of Culture’s first communication on culture will not provide the Stortinget with the most appropriate basis for a thorough debate on the further development of the cultural school, and that there is a need for a separate communication on the subject of the Ministry of Knowledge. The members of the Committee of the Right and Advance Party feel that it is important to contribute to the strengthening of the work of the cultural school and to facilitate the provision of good cultural ballast and competence to the schools of culture. These members indicate that it is an important principle that municipalities as owners of the cultural schools must be able to determine the content and scope of the offer themselves and that room must be provided for local adaptations. These members believe it is natural that the strengthening of cultural schools is seen in the context of the government's announced cultural message and is given its natural place in it.
