But you can't pretend that we're still in 2014. So I would like to add a comment to what the representative Lysbakken said, for what is the most important thing that has happened since October? Well, it's that we've launched the plan for job renewal throughout the school. All courses at school are to be renewed. And what we've done for that process if it's not just professional trust, both by having had a long process – for which we've been criticized, for some, I shouldn't mention party names, has meant that we should do it faster – and an open process, which is going into every single classroom. Not only organisations, but all teachers, should be involved. Not only do professionals sit in a room and decide how the curricula should be. What if not a process based just on trust that the profession itself will be involved in developing what is the school of the future? Then there has never been anyone who has thought that we should not have educational freedom. This was the case before, because the old curricula did not build on educational freedom as a principle. The basic principle came too fully into the school curriculum with the Knowledge Promise. The L97 syllabuses were abolished, including: and guided the methods of teachers, e.g. group work directives. There is no one, at least not from the government parties, who believes that the new curricula should not be based on full freedom of method. But I believe that there is a crucial difference between that which, if you read it a little maliciously, is the proposal, or the point, from the SV and the Center Party, which says that the professional teacher has procedural responsibility and gives a large professional space for action, and what you're talking about, which is professional silence. Professional stillness is something other than the fact that the individual teacher is king in his own classroom. I don't want us to create curricula that standardise teaching methods in Norway, but of course the profession has a responsibility to keep up with research, disseminate good teaching methods, learn from each other and also to criticise each other's teaching. Fagene Norwegian, English and mathematics, it's not that important, said the representative Tingelstad Wøien. First of all, it's not these subjects that we measure in the national tests, it's reading, writing and billing. No matter how practical your profession is, you need reading, writing and billing. So you might think that you're going to turn the national samples into sample samples, but what you lose then is the possibility for the teacher, for the school, for the principal, for the local politicians to use these tests. What we do in school is just based on trust in the profession. I want to mention two tracks that we follow. The first is this with red tape. We have simplified the reporting already since the autumn of 2015. We have made changes to the legislation so that we reduce the requirements for written in-flight assessment to a minimum. Unlike previous governments, we have actually removed a compulsory test from school. It was a screening test on Vg1 that was ‘nice to know’, but not necessary to have, and then we take it away. In addition, we have reviewed all the written documentation requirements relating to national supervision. Then we've worked with KS to bring this down to the municipalities. And as I said earlier, too, except for the perfectly obvious things where we don't agree, for example. on national tests: I would like to welcome very specific measures to address several legislative changes from the Storting, time thieves that are thought to be removed. One of the suggestions we received in a report was to remove the student survey. I'm simply against that. I think it gives us important information. The second is that trust also needs to be built in a different way. We have contributed, for example: The teacher role selection that we put down. On the basis of this, the KS and the Education Association, the largest teacher organisation, have built a process that is just about how the professionals and the school owners will discuss together, understanding that they disagree, but also build processes based on trust. I am more than happy to discuss how to both red tape, build trust and build quality in school – all for which I am – but I think with respect to report that it gets a little funny if it's like everything you're against in politics is mistrust, and everything you're for is automatic trust. It is a misrepresentation of a debate that is too important for it.

ParlaMint-NO_2017-06-13.ana.ud206e9389	I would like to thank Stortinget for accepting the representative proposal that SV has made. More recent research, the Children’s Ombudsman’s technical report ‘No Objective and Meaning?’ and Statped’s report ‘Near-school principle and inclusion of children with special training needs in primary school’ confirm that Norwegian schools are not able to provide an equivalent training offer to all pupils and that both the scale and quality of the offer vary widely. If all students are to receive the training they need and require, special educational skills must be increased in schools and in municipalities. Regular training needs to be strengthened in order to adapt far more closely to the different needs of students and to assess the types of special educational measures that can support an inclusive learning environment. The situation now appears to be that lack of special education is not compensated for by additional measures in regular training. There must be an assessment of the best way to support the learner’s learning process, which must lead to the type of action chosen and not a quantitative measure of reduced special education. In the same way, it must be an objective that ordinary teaching can be organised in such a way that pupils who are not currently receiving a satisfactory benefit from the teaching can receive the training they need within the framework of the community. Then we're back to the question of increasing teacher density, which we mean, in combination with special pedagogical competence, can strengthen the school's ability to maintain an inclusive learning environment. It may also counteract an unfortunate practice in which the pursuit of diagnostics, endless investigations and the proportion of individual decisions on special education determine the type of resources available to the school. We have seen that the Minister of Knowledge repeatedly refers to research results in proving that teaching density does not affect students' learning. There may be a point to point out that research is quite consistent with the fact that increasing teacher density is important for students with special needs. In addition, the scheme for increasing teacher density in upper secondary schools showed that primary school credits increased and special education was reduced in the schools covered by the scheme. It also underlines that winding up the plan was a wrong decision. Special educational competence in school is important in order to provide adapted training for all pupils, not just students with different types of disabilities or specific learning difficulties. This type of competence can also help to identify the learning challenges of students at the earliest possible stage and is therefore important for what we have talked about earlier this evening, early efforts. During the red-green government in 2011, the big message on learning and community was promoted. It was the first large, comprehensive notification of the special educational training provision in kindergarten and school of 20 years and resulted in a major reorganisation of the state special educational support device. There has been an important contribution to strengthening the provision of services to students in need of Statped services. ParlaMint-NO_2017-06-13.ana.ud206e9480	This representative proposal from SV proposes inter alia: putting in place municipal pilot projects for a trust-based management model for the care of the elderly. It's an excellent idea that the Workers' Party supports. We have actually programmed that we want to try a trust reform in as many municipalities as possible. Trust reform is not about ‘ensuring trust’, but about concrete organisational measures combined with gradual cultural change. We can pick up our experiences, for example: from the municipality of Copenhagen. Copenhagen formally decided in 2013 to introduce a trust reform, and health and care came first. The agreement was concluded with five employee organisations on the principles of such a reform. The previous government in Denmark estimated that the savings would be about five billion through such a grip. The reform has produced results in Denmark. It has inspired politicians here, too, especially in the Red-Green parties. In 2015, Oslo City Council decided to try out a trust reform in four urban areas. Tromsø is also running a test project. Trust reform is a kind of up-down pyramid where management facilitates more responsibility for users and the first-line, based on economic autonomy within adopted budgets, surfaces structure with fewer and more involved leaders and cleaning up rules and reporting procedures. The Labour Party believes that people receiving care services should be able to decide for themselves on important parts of the services received, within defined limits. The trust reform of bureaucratises the care of the elderly and gives greater responsibilities and trust in the interaction between employees and the recipients. It must be allowed to think: what if the government had been in favour of a trust reform, instead of throwing the money out of the window through the so-called state financing of municipal care services? Very many municipalities, including civil servants, withdrew early from this attempt, because it was calculated that the municipality would lose a lot of money to participate. Only six municipalities remain. For all over-measurements, it may not be possible to measure the efficacy of the test, because the test group is too small. Researchers from Telemark Research, quoted last year in the Municipal Report, point out that there must be at least 20 municipalities to provide valid and reasonable answers. KS has stated that the experiment is considered worthless. But now, five to twelve, the government is launching an 'elders' reform, which is supposed to start listening to the municipalities, something you haven't done so far. Yes, it is also claimed that the reform should support activity, experience and nutrition – i.e. In contrast to what has shaped the government's old age policy so far. ParlaMint-NO_2017-06-13.ana.ud206e9552	Two years ago, Bergen decided to streamline the use of time, the so-called norm times, with caregivers. It was a rather cross-political decision. Some examples were that the morning seats for those in need of care were reduced by 10 minutes, from 60 to 50 minutes. Shower time was cut by 10 minutes to 35 minutes. Tasks such as eye drops, writing a shopping list or helping with support stockings were also reduced by 5 minutes each. Household cleaning was to be done every three weeks instead of every other week. An evaluation report has now been drawn up by the care centres to establish that the new norm times have had more negative consequences. 86 pst. of those added to the home services believed that the changes had gone beyond the users, and they added that the working day had been more hectic. 90 pst. by the home nurses believed that the working day had been worse. The results should not be so eye-catching. It's something everyone can understand. ParlaMint-NO_2017-06-13.ana.ud206e9737	The Christian People's Party has put forward proposals to provide undocumented immigrants with access to prevention and treatment services in primary care services, including the right to be on the list of permanent staff. It is also suggested that the government ensure that the supply of vulnerable groups of paperless migrants, such as children, persons with disabilities, pregnant women and the elderly, is in line with the supply of the population otherwise. It is also proposed to ask the government to develop funding schemes for health services for paperless without funds. Suggestions cannot be plural, but they are supported by the Christian People's Party, the Left and the Center parties in the option, with slightly different wording. The right to health is a human right. It is indisputable and is enshrined in several international conventions and declarations. Health care should be available to all, without discrimination. In Norway, people without permanent residence in the empire are entitled to immediate health care, health care that is absolutely necessary and cannot be expected, necessary health care before and after birth and abortion and the right to infectious disease protection. Children also have the right to the necessary health and care services from the local health service and the specialist services. However, patients who are not resident in Norway should cover the treatment and care costs themselves. If the patient is unable to do so, the costs shall be borne by the healthcare institution or service provider concerned. There are many indications that the right to health care in Norway without discrimination is not followed up in practice – not because the services or professionals oppose it, but because the Stortinget uses health services as an immigration regulatory measure. That's inappropriate. In 2015, eleven Norwegian organisations signed a call for health care for paperless people. It was suggested that children under the Children’s Convention must have the right to permanent residence as other children, and it was focused on the fact that today’s legislation places health professionals at a disadvantage when they are forced to deviate from the principle of providing care and medical treatment based on needs. It violates the occupational ethics guidelines for health professionals. In the May 2009 Health Directorate report, Migration and Health, they make a number of recommendations to us to improve access to health care for paperless migrants. There are some professional contributions that support this proposal from the Norwegian Medical Association, the Church's Bymition, the Doctors Without Borders and Health Aid for Paperless in Oslo and Bergen, all of which describe challenges with today's practice. It is alarming that a health council and a population increase can argue that a health assistance should not be provided because it is too generous a scheme, which could be a migration factor that will lead to more people coming to Norway as undocumented migrants. The centre part will be clear: doctors and other health professionals should be health workers, not border guards. With this, I'm going to deal with the proposals of the Center Party, what we're all about, and the ones we're dealing with.

ParlaMint-NO_2017-06-13.ana.ud206e9772	In Document 8, the proposers have several proposals that give people without the right to stay in Norway, so-called paperless, extended health care rights, which are in line with the health care of the general public. This applies, inter alia, to: prevention and curative services in primary health services, such as the right to be on the list of permanent staff. Many have been concerned with health care for paperless people, and in 2015 11 Norwegian organisations signed the Right to health care for paperless people. The UN Committee on Economic, Social and Cultural Rights recommends that people without permanent residence in the state receive health care in the same way as others. The regulation on the right to health and care services for persons without permanent residence in the state states that everyone has the right to immediate assistance. It is also established that undocumented persons are entitled to unwaitable health care, the right to necessary health care before and after birth, the right to abortion and the right to infectious diseases services. Children are also entitled to health and care services from municipalities and specialist health services, as are people who are mentally unstable. Children and pregnant women have the same right to health care if their stay is lawful or not. This is in line with the UN Convention on the Rights of the Child, which requires that the child’s best interests be a fundamental consideration in the design of the offer. The right to health care was also strengthened and regulated in such a way that there was a consistent practice in 2011. Since pregnant persons without paper are not members of the public interest, they have to pay for the service under the legislation if they have the means to do so, but if they do not have the means to cover the expenditure themselves, they must be paid by the municipality or the State. Comments from the Christian People's Party, the Centre Party and the Left show that schemes in other countries are more accessible than Norwegian ones. But it is quite difficult to compare, and it is up to each country to decide which healthcare to provide within the framework of human rights. It is important that the legislation we have on health care for paperless people does not break our international obligations. Today, we have legislation that distinguishes between those who have legal residence and those who do not have legal residence. Right does not want to change that and cannot support the proposals. We are also sceptical about our own system of financing health care for the paperless, as it can also create unintended effects.

We live in a legal society where we have good legal practices about these kinds of decisions. I expect that the individual will also deal with this – with the decisions of the Norwegian authorities.

The overall level of reporting and documentation requirements at national and local level must be set at a level that does not cost the student’s time and the professional space for action. Objectives and quality indicators shall reflect the broad knowledge view of the school. Learning-promoting governance principles are used as the basis for the quality assessment of the school. Real participation and trust shall characterise cooperation between school owners, school managers and teachers at all levels. Strong professional communities shall be the cornerstone of the school's professional work and development. The professional teacher has procedural responsibilities and a wide range of professional areas of action.’ Proposal No. 3 sounds: ‘The government asks the government to propose measures for the quality assessment of schools that discourage increased competition and the ranking of schools.’
